  ## Introduction
  
  For many people who menstruate, disposable sanitary pads are an essential product that they rely on every month, but few have stopped to think about the history of disposable sanitary pads, how it is made, and the impact it has on female health, the environment, and society. From ancient times to the present day, the development of menstrual products has been shaped by cultural taboos, medical advancements, and consumer demand.
  
  Menstruation is the monthly shedding of the uterine lining, which typically occurs during days 1–5 of the menstrual cycle. The uterine lining is composed of blood, tissue, fluid, and mucus, collectively known as menstrual fluid \[ [1](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR1 "Madhu C, Patel B (2024) Designing pH-responsive SAP-coated menstrual pads and comparing them to marketable products through quality analysis. Bull Natl Res Cent 48:41.                    https://doi.org/10.1186/s42269-024-01196-0                                    ")\]. Girls typically experience menarche between the ages of 12 and 13, and menstruation usually continues until women reach menopause between the ages of 45 and 55, with an average of 37.5 years of menstruation \[ [2](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR2 "Kavinkumar MC, Saravanakumar A, Parthiban P, Mohanraj KS, Sangeetha S, Periyanayaki P, Harsath MJ, Ayyappan K (2023) Sanitary towels, their menace, and the ministration of herbalism: an overview of the feminine pad patron mad mady. Int J Innov Res Technol 10:1026–1040")\]. On average, a woman will use 12 000 single-use menstrual sanitary products to manage it during this time \[ [3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR3 "Hand J, Hwang C, Vogel W, Lopez C, Hwang S (2023) An exploration of market organic sanitary products for improving menstrual health and environmental impact. J Water Sanit Hyg Dev 13:63–77.                    https://doi.org/10.2166/washdev.2023.020                                    ")\]. The Daily Maverick published an article where it was reported that 52 400 tonnes of sanitary waste are disposed of per year in South Africa \[ [4](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR4 "Moodley N (2023) R30-billion: sanitary products bleeding women dry even though cheaper, greener options available. Daily Maverick Business Reflection")\]. A study done in the UK concluded that 28 114 tonnes of menstrual waste are produced annually in their country, of which 26 903 tonnes are from disposable products \[ [5](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR5 "Blair LAG, Bajón -Fernández Y, Villa R (2022) An exploratory study of the impact and potential of menstrual hygiene management waste in the UK. Clean Eng Technol 7:100435.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.clet.2022.100435                                    ")\]. According to Businesswaste.co.uk, this is 4.3 billion disposable products \[ [6](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR6 "Matthews G (2025) Sanitary waste facts. In: businesswste.co.uk")\]. The menstrual cycle involves a series of changes in a woman's body that prepare it for pregnancy, which are controlled by hormones. To manage menstruation, the menstrual fluid flow must be captured, which can be achieved using various sanitary products such as tampons, disposable sanitary pads, menstrual cups, and reusable sanitary pads and panties \[ [7](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR7 "Upson K, Shearston JA, Kioumourtzoglou M-A (2022) Menstrual products as a source of environmental chemical exposure: a review from the epidemiologic perspective. Curr Environ Health Rep 9:38–52.                    https://doi.org/10.1007/s40572-022-00331-1                                    ")\].
  
  Menstrual health management (MHM) is a crucial aspect of reproductive health that has not been adequately addressed to date. It refers to the practices and products that women use to manage their menstruation. This includes having access to sanitary products, as well as proper hygiene practices like washing hands and the genital area with soap and water during menstruation \[ [8](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR8 "Kambala C, Chinangwa A, Chipeta E, Torondel B, Morse T (2020) Acceptability of menstrual products interventions for menstrual hygiene management among women and girls in Malawi. Reprod Health 17:185.                    https://doi.org/10.1186/s12978-020-01045-z                                    ")\]. Many women are still confronted with obstacles preventing them from having a dignified and comfortable menstruation experience such as not having access to clean water, washing facilities, and appropriately priced sanitary products \[ [9](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR9 "Kaur N, Byard RW (2021) Menstrual health management: practices, challenges and human rights violations. Med Leg J 89:241–246.                    https://doi.org/10.1177/00258172211052111                                    ")\], especially those in rural or low socio-economic households \[ [8](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR8 "Kambala C, Chinangwa A, Chipeta E, Torondel B, Morse T (2020) Acceptability of menstrual products interventions for menstrual hygiene management among women and girls in Malawi. Reprod Health 17:185.                    https://doi.org/10.1186/s12978-020-01045-z                                    ")\]. If these difficulties cannot be managed by women, it leads to irritation, distress, and discomfort which affects their physical, mental, and social well-being, education, and their income-generating opportunities \[ [10](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR10 "Hennegan J, Winkler IT, Bobel C, Keiser D, Hampton J, Larsson G, Chandra-Mouli V, Plesons M, Mahon T (2021) Menstrual health: a definition for policy, practice, and research. Sex Reprod Health Matters 29:31–38.                    https://doi.org/10.1080/26410397.2021.1911618                                    ")\].
  
  Torondel _et. al._ found that women who do not practice frequent personal washing and use reusable absorbent materials are more likely to suffer from infections such as yeast ( _Candida albicans)_ infection \[ [11](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR11 "Torondel B, Sinha S, Mohanty JR, Swain T, Sahoo P, Panda B, Nayak A, Bara M, Bilung B, Cumming O, Panigrahi P, Das P (2018) Association between unhygienic menstrual management practices and prevalence of lower reproductive tract infections: a hospital-based cross-sectional study in Odisha. India BMC Infect Dis 18:473.                    https://doi.org/10.1186/s12879-018-3384-2                                    ")\]. Therefore, women should be thoughtful about their menstrual hygiene practices to avoid various infections, such as vaginal, reproductive tract, or urinary tract infections, and even cervical cancer \[ [12](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR12 "Banappagoudar SB, Kripa N, Kanna KR, Kurian NK, Mayank R (2021) Anti-bacterial sanitary napkin using biomaterial application. Volatiles Essent Oils 8:12254–12263"), [13](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR13 "World Health Organization Menstrual hygiene management.                    https://www.who.int/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/menstrual-hygiene-management                                    ")\]. The MHM movement advocates for women to be able to afford and choose resources and materials that they prefer and feel comfortable using. It advocates for women to feel safe during menses. MHM is an issue, that if properly addressed, can improve global health, sustainable development goals, human rights equalities, and gender equalities \[ [10](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR10 "Hennegan J, Winkler IT, Bobel C, Keiser D, Hampton J, Larsson G, Chandra-Mouli V, Plesons M, Mahon T (2021) Menstrual health: a definition for policy, practice, and research. Sex Reprod Health Matters 29:31–38.                    https://doi.org/10.1080/26410397.2021.1911618                                    ")\].
  
  Educating girls about their bodies, menstrual needs, and addressing social and cultural taboos surrounding menstruation and its management \[ [14](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR14 "Sommer M, Hirsch JS, Nathanson C, Parker RG (2015) Comfortably, safely, and without shame: defining menstrual hygiene management as a public health issue. Am J Public Health 105:1302–1311.                    https://doi.org/10.2105/AJPH                                    ")\] is crucial as it enables individuals to make informed decisions that can promote their menstrual health and well-being \[ [10](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR10 "Hennegan J, Winkler IT, Bobel C, Keiser D, Hampton J, Larsson G, Chandra-Mouli V, Plesons M, Mahon T (2021) Menstrual health: a definition for policy, practice, and research. Sex Reprod Health Matters 29:31–38.                    https://doi.org/10.1080/26410397.2021.1911618                                    ")\]. However, a topic that is neglected by educational programs is the potential negative effects of sanitary pads on women’s health and the environment.
  
  Women are exposed to menstrual hygiene products for more than 3500 days during their lives. Very few people stop to consider the effect of such long-term exposure to phthalates and other harmful chemicals still found in some sanitary pads, neither do they consider the effect of their disposal on the environment. Unlike the pharmaceutical and food industry, menstrual hygiene material manufacturers do not disclose chemical/material information on their packaging \[ [3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR3 "Hand J, Hwang C, Vogel W, Lopez C, Hwang S (2023) An exploration of market organic sanitary products for improving menstrual health and environmental impact. J Water Sanit Hyg Dev 13:63–77.                    https://doi.org/10.2166/washdev.2023.020                                    "), [15](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR15 "Nicole W (2014) A Question for women’s health: chemicals in feminine hygiene products and personal lubricants. Environ Health Perspect 122:A70–A75.                    https://doi.org/10.1289/ehp.122-A70                                    "), [16](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR16 "Desmedt B, Marcelis Q, Zhilivoda D, Deconinck E (2020) Sensitizing fragrances in absorbent hygiene products. Contact Dermatitis 82:279–282.                    https://doi.org/10.1111/cod.13472                                    ")\]. This lack of transparency causes consumers to be unaware of the potential health risks and environmental health risks of these materials, especially for sanitary pads. Sanitary pads contain much more than just cotton, it contains synthetic materials (polyethylene and polypropylene), glues, superabsorbent gels/polymers, and fragrances to name a few \[ [17](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR17 "Woeller KE, Hochwalt AE (2015) Safety assessment of sanitary pads with a polymeric foam absorbent core. Regul Toxicol Pharmacol 73:419–424.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.yrtph.2015.07.028                                    ")\]. These materials contain dioxins \[ [18](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR18 "Shin JH, Ahn YG (2007) Analysis of polychlorinated dibenzo-p-dioxins and dibenzo-furans in sanitary products of women. Text Res J 77:597–603.                    https://doi.org/10.1177/0040517507078786                                    ")\], volatile organic chemicals (VOCs) \[ [3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR3 "Hand J, Hwang C, Vogel W, Lopez C, Hwang S (2023) An exploration of market organic sanitary products for improving menstrual health and environmental impact. J Water Sanit Hyg Dev 13:63–77.                    https://doi.org/10.2166/washdev.2023.020                                    ")\], phthalates, parabens, bisphenols \[ [19](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR19 "Park CJ, Barakat R, Ulanov A, Li Z, Lin PC, Chiu K, Zhou S, Perez P, Lee J, Flaws J, Ko CMJ (2019) Sanitary pads and diapers contain higher phthalate contents than those in common commercial plastic products. Reprod Toxicol 84:114–121.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.reprotox.2019.01.005                                    ")\], and fluorides \[ [20](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR20 "Zhou Y, Lin X, Xing Y, Zhang X, Lee HK, Huang Z (2023) Per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances in personal hygiene products: the implications for human exposure and emission to the environment. Environ Sci Technol 57:8484–8495.                    https://doi.org/10.1021/acs.est.2c08912                                    ")\] that are then released into sanitary pads, which exposes the wearer. All these chemicals have the potential to cause adverse effects on women’s health and the health of the environment \[ [3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR3 "Hand J, Hwang C, Vogel W, Lopez C, Hwang S (2023) An exploration of market organic sanitary products for improving menstrual health and environmental impact. J Water Sanit Hyg Dev 13:63–77.                    https://doi.org/10.2166/washdev.2023.020                                    "), [18](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR18 "Shin JH, Ahn YG (2007) Analysis of polychlorinated dibenzo-p-dioxins and dibenzo-furans in sanitary products of women. Text Res J 77:597–603.                    https://doi.org/10.1177/0040517507078786                                    "), [21](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR21 "Weuve J, Hauser R, Calafat AM, Missmer SA, Wise LA (2010) Association of exposure to phthalates with endometriosis and uterine leiomyomata: findings from NHANES, 1999–2004. Environ Health Perspect 118:825–832.                    https://doi.org/10.1289/ehp.0901543                                    "), [22](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR22 "Ferguson KK, Rosen EM, Rosario Z, Feric Z, Calafat AM, McElrath TF, Vélez Vega C, Cordero JF, Alshawabkeh A, Meeker JD (2019) Environmental phthalate exposure and preterm birth in the PROTECT birth cohort. Environ Int 132:105099.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.envint.2019.105099                                    "), [23](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR23 "Kim HY, Lee JD, Kim J-Y, Lee JY, Bae O-N, Choi Y-K, Baek E, Kang S, Min C, Seo K, Choi K, Lee B-M, Kim K-B (2019) Risk assessment of volatile organic compounds (VOCs) detected in sanitary pads. J Toxicol Environ Health A 82:678–695.                    https://doi.org/10.1080/15287394.2019.1642607                                    ")\]. Failure to address this topic and raise awareness about it, it prevents women from making informed decisions about their genital health and their impact on the environment.
  
  Over the past decade, there has been an increase in research on disposable sanitary pads, as shown in Fig. [1](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Fig1). Many of these studies focus on developing new and more affordable pads using natural and biodegradable fibres that are locally available. Others explore the relationship between culture and the use of sanitary products, as well as how different products affect women’s lives and health. Conducting such research is crucial as it can lead to the development of better, healthier, and more suitable products for various users.
  
  **Figure 1**
  
  ![Figure 1](https://media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1007%2Fs10853-025-11151-7/MediaObjects/10853_2025_11151_Fig1_HTML.png)The alternative text for this image may have been generated using AI.
  
  [Full size image](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7/figures/1)
  
  A graph showing the steady increase in research articles published containing the term “disposable sanitary pads” on Google Scholar.
  
  In this article, the composition of modern pads is examined, the various issues associated with their use are discussed, including potential health risks, environmental concerns, economic disparities, and societal stigmas. We will also discuss the current research on sanitary pads with the potential to provide sustainable, healthy, and equitable solutions.
  
  ## Composition and structure of disposable sanitary pads
  
  From cloth rags to a carefully planned out layered pad, disposable sanitary pads have been redesigned time after time to reach the general design template used today by almost every absorbent hygiene product (AHP) manufacturer (see Fig. [2](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Fig2) and Table [1](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Tab1)). The design has been created with a focus on comfort, superior absorption, and vaginal health. Many countries have established manufacturing standards that AHPs must comply with for them to be sold to the public. However, although these specific standards and designs ensure compliance, the various materials/ingredients used may not necessarily be good for the health of women and girls.
  
  **Figure 2**
  
  ![Figure 2](https://media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1007%2Fs10853-025-11151-7/MediaObjects/10853_2025_11151_Fig2_HTML.png)The alternative text for this image may have been generated using AI.
  
  [Full size image](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7/figures/2)
  
  General structure of absorbent hygiene products.
  
  **Table 1 The different layers in disposable sanitary pads \[ [17](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR17 "Woeller KE, Hochwalt AE (2015) Safety assessment of sanitary pads with a polymeric foam absorbent core. Regul Toxicol Pharmacol 73:419–424.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.yrtph.2015.07.028                                    ")\]**
  
  [Full size table](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7/tables/1)
  
  A recent Turkish study conducted on women’s expectations from disposable sanitary pads found that the most preferred performance characteristics of menstrual products are leakage prevention and absorption ability \[ [24](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR24 "Kara S (2021) A research study about the expectations from sanitary napkins, current problems and design of a functional sanitary napkin. J Inst Sci Technol 37:74–90")\]. Thereafter, sense of dryness, soft surface, odour prevention, flexibility, and breathability were also preferred. When choosing menstrual products, the participants would first choose based on the performance properties, then the material that the product is made of, and finally, biodegradability would be considered. Price was one of the least considered options for choosing a product. Some of the biggest problems experienced by the participants were leakage, the sense of wetness, development of skin rash, and insufficient adhesion to underwear. The most popular additive women would prefer in sanitary napkins is antibacterial properties. The main reason the participants would consider pads made of natural material is that they believe it will be less harmful to their skin and that it will be biodegradable. Many of the participants suspect that there are harmful chemicals in current pads, indicating that the respondents do not trust disposable sanitary pads in terms of their health \[ [24](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR24 "Kara S (2021) A research study about the expectations from sanitary napkins, current problems and design of a functional sanitary napkin. J Inst Sci Technol 37:74–90")\]. The structural composition (see Fig. [3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Fig3)) \[ [25](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR25 "Bashari A, Rouhani Shirvan A, Shakeri M (2018) Cellulose-based hydrogels for personal care products. Polym Adv Technol 29:2853–2867")\] of a sanitary pad plays an important role in all these characteristics.
  
  **Figure 3**
  
  ![Figure 3](https://media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1007%2Fs10853-025-11151-7/MediaObjects/10853_2025_11151_Fig3_HTML.png)The alternative text for this image may have been generated using AI.
  
  [Full size image](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7/figures/3)
  
  The composition of ultra-thin sanitary pads \[ [12](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR12 "Banappagoudar SB, Kripa N, Kanna KR, Kurian NK, Mayank R (2021) Anti-bacterial sanitary napkin using biomaterial application. Volatiles Essent Oils 8:12254–12263")\]
  
  The female genital tract has evolved to become self-cleaning; therefore, the vaginal tract contains many blood vessels and produces mucus to wash away dirt and harmful organisms \[ [15](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR15 "Nicole W (2014) A Question for women’s health: chemicals in feminine hygiene products and personal lubricants. Environ Health Perspect 122:A70–A75.                    https://doi.org/10.1289/ehp.122-A70                                    ")\]. Since the vaginal canal is lined with a mucous membrane rich in blood vessels, it absorbs at a higher rate than normal skin, as do some of the external parts of a female’s genitals. This absorption is to such a high extent that researchers have even explored the possibility of administering drugs through the vagina and vulva, due to the high water permeability of the skin and since it rapidly absorbs chemicals without metabolizing them \[ [15](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR15 "Nicole W (2014) A Question for women’s health: chemicals in feminine hygiene products and personal lubricants. Environ Health Perspect 122:A70–A75.                    https://doi.org/10.1289/ehp.122-A70                                    ")\]. Hence, the design of the sanitary pad is very important in preventing rewet/reflux of the fluid (the transfer of fluid back to the skin from the inner layers of a sanitary pad) with dissolved pad components that can be absorbed by the sensitive skin lining the genitals.
  
  The vagina requires a pH below 5 to provide it with the necessary protection against pathogens/infections, as an acidic pH prevents the growth of harmful microorganisms \[ [26](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR26 "Ding C, Yu Y, Zhou Q (2021) Bacterial vaginosis: effects on reproduction and its therapeutics. J Gynecol Obstet Hum Reprod 50:102174.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jogoh.2021.102174                                    ")\]. The bacterial flora in healthy women produce lactic acid maintaining a low pH \[ [27](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR27 "Sauperl O, Zabret A, Fras Zemljič L (2020) Development of advanced sanitary materials with the use of probiotic paste. J Eng Fiber Fabr 15:155892502092221.                    https://doi.org/10.1177/1558925020922215                                    ")\]. The production of H2O2 by the vagina’s bacterial flora also protects it from infections \[ [28](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR28 "Eschenbach DA, Thwin SS, Patton DL, Hooton TM, Stapleton AE, Agnew K, Winter C, Meier A, Stamm WE (2000) Influence of the normal menstrual cycle on vaginal tissue, discharge, and microflora. Clin Infect Dis 30:901–907.                    https://doi.org/10.1086/313818                                    "), [29](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR29 "Miko E, Barakonyi A (2023) The role of hydrogen-peroxide (H2O2) produced by vaginal microbiota in female reproductive health. Antioxidants 12:1055.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/antiox12051055                                    ")\]. The use of antibiotics or antimycotics, hormonal changes, menstruation, pregnancy, menopause, diabetes, oral contraception, weakened immune system, and excessive hygiene can break the pH balance of the vagina. An increase in the pH (which usually indicates an overgrowth of Gram-negative bacteria \[ [3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR3 "Hand J, Hwang C, Vogel W, Lopez C, Hwang S (2023) An exploration of market organic sanitary products for improving menstrual health and environmental impact. J Water Sanit Hyg Dev 13:63–77.                    https://doi.org/10.2166/washdev.2023.020                                    ")\]) creates a favourable environment for the growth of microorganisms, making it susceptible for infections \[ [27](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR27 "Sauperl O, Zabret A, Fras Zemljič L (2020) Development of advanced sanitary materials with the use of probiotic paste. J Eng Fiber Fabr 15:155892502092221.                    https://doi.org/10.1177/1558925020922215                                    ")\]. During the menstrual cycle, the pH of the vagina is disrupted and increases with the outflow of menstrual fluid and blood, which are neutral \[ [27](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR27 "Sauperl O, Zabret A, Fras Zemljič L (2020) Development of advanced sanitary materials with the use of probiotic paste. J Eng Fiber Fabr 15:155892502092221.                    https://doi.org/10.1177/1558925020922215                                    "), [28](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR28 "Eschenbach DA, Thwin SS, Patton DL, Hooton TM, Stapleton AE, Agnew K, Winter C, Meier A, Stamm WE (2000) Influence of the normal menstrual cycle on vaginal tissue, discharge, and microflora. Clin Infect Dis 30:901–907.                    https://doi.org/10.1086/313818                                    ")\]. Thus, it is important for the menstrual fluid to be removed from the surface of the genital skin, and for the components of a sanitary pad not to interfere further with the vaginal pH and flora.
  
  ### Top sheet
  
  The top sheet of absorbent pads is typically made of perforated nonwoven polyethylene (PE) or polypropylene (PP). This layer is designed to quickly transfer liquid to the absorbent layers beneath, so a high wicking ability is desirable for this layer \[ [30](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR30 "Bae J, Kwon H, Kim J (2018) Safety evaluation of absorbent hygiene pads: a review on assessment framework and test methods. Sustainability 10:4146.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/su10114146                                    ")\]. A high humidity, caused by poor wicking ability of the top sheet, between the top sheet and the wearers skin, together with shear forces, can cause dermatitis \[ [31](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR31 "Tang KPM, Li CH, Kan CW (2020) Instrumentation for measuring the wet frictional property of sanitary pads. Fibers Polym 21:216–223.                    https://doi.org/10.1007/s12221-020-9623-3                                    ")\]. Often, an emollient is added to the top sheet to prevent skin irritation \[ [30](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR30 "Bae J, Kwon H, Kim J (2018) Safety evaluation of absorbent hygiene pads: a review on assessment framework and test methods. Sustainability 10:4146.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/su10114146                                    ")\], however, the very thing added to prevent irritation also has the ability to provoke allergies \[ [32](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR32 "Dixit S, Sharma M (2023) Benign inflammatory vulval dermatoses: diagnostic approach and management strategies. Obstet Gynaecol Reprod Med 33:141–149.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ogrm.2023.02.004                                    ")\]. PE and PP are the textile of choice in hygiene materials as some of their characteristics include chemical stability and softness. Viscose rayon is also used in medical and hygiene materials primarily because it is hydrophilic and has a similar absorbency to that of wood pulp. It is a man-made fibre regenerated from cellulose fibres from the cellulose xanthate process, and is generally blended with PP fibres \[ [33](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR33 "Kakonke G, Tesfaye T, Sithole B, Ntunka M (2019) Review on the manufacturing and properties of nonwoven superabsorbent core fabrics used in disposable diapers. Int J Chem Sci 17:302.                    https://doi.org/10.21767/0972-768X.1000302                                    ")\].
  
  A study on the physicochemical and morphological properties of the top layers of six commercially available pads was done in order to characterize them \[ [34](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR34 "Pohlmann M (2016) Design and materials selection: analysis of similar sanitary pads for daily use. J Eng Res Appl 6:74–79")\]. The results confirmed the presence of polypropylene in five of the six pads, and cellulose in two of them, in the top layer. The top layers were made from nonwoven fabric produced using spunbond (a process of converting a polymer into continuous filaments) and point bonding (a process of binding the filaments by applying heat and pressure). Contaminants (yellow circles) and cotton fibres (yellow arrows) were also found to be present in most of the samples (see Fig. [4](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Fig4)). This research sheds light on the design and manufacturing process of materials used in the top layer of some disposable sanitary pads, which can be used as a basis for future investigations to develop new and improved intimate hygiene products \[ [34](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR34 "Pohlmann M (2016) Design and materials selection: analysis of similar sanitary pads for daily use. J Eng Res Appl 6:74–79")\].
  
  **Figure 4**
  
  ![Figure 4](https://media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1007%2Fs10853-025-11151-7/MediaObjects/10853_2025_11151_Fig4_HTML.jpg)The alternative text for this image may have been generated using AI.

  Scanning electron micrograph of the top layer of a sanitary pad \[ [34](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR34 "Pohlmann M (2016) Design and materials selection: analysis of similar sanitary pads for daily use. J Eng Res Appl 6:74–79")\]. Reprinted from the International Journal of Engineering Research and Application, 6, M. Pohlmann, Design and Materials Selection: analysis of similar sanitary pads for daily use, 74–79. IJERA is an open access journal under the Creative Commons Agreement.
  
  ### Acquisition layer
  
  The second layer, which is an optional layer, is the acquisition layer. This layer is typically made of cellulose and nonwoven polyester and is designed to facilitate the spread of menstrual fluid evenly across the entire area of the sanitary pad. It also prevents the fluid from moving back to the top sheet by transferring it quickly to the absorbent layer beneath \[ [35](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR35 "Liu H, Feng Y, Qian X (2019) Preparation and properties of eccentric hollow fiber nonwovens for acquisition distribution layer. J Eng Fiber Fabr 14:155892501988549.                    https://doi.org/10.1177/1558925019885497                                    ")\].
  
  Figure [5](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Fig5) shows surface and cross-section SEM images of through-air bonded ethylene-propylene nonwoven fibres studied by Liu _et. al._ \[ [35](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR35 "Liu H, Feng Y, Qian X (2019) Preparation and properties of eccentric hollow fiber nonwovens for acquisition distribution layer. J Eng Fiber Fabr 14:155892501988549.                    https://doi.org/10.1177/1558925019885497                                    ")\]. The fluid penetrates the top sheet after which it spreads uniformly across the area of the sanitary pad by the wicking effect of the acquisition layer. This prevents the pooling of fluid against the user’s skin, reducing leakage and providing dryness for the user. The ideal nonwoven fabric used for this layer should have fast liquid penetration and diffusion. Nowadays through-air bonded nonwovens are considered better alternatives than spunbonded nonwovens. For visual effects, nonwovens of different colours are used in the acquisition layer \[ [35](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR35 "Liu H, Feng Y, Qian X (2019) Preparation and properties of eccentric hollow fiber nonwovens for acquisition distribution layer. J Eng Fiber Fabr 14:155892501988549.                    https://doi.org/10.1177/1558925019885497                                    ")\]. Nonwoven materials are the key to effective and efficient absorption in absorbent hygiene products. The benefits of using nonwoven materials are that it offers excellent absorption, provide softness, smoothness, stretch ability, strength, good strike-through, tear resistance, and breathability \[ [36](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR36 "Venkataraman D, Shabani E, Park JH (2023) Advancement of nonwoven fabrics in personal protective equipment. Materials 16:3964.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/ma16113964                                    "), [37](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR37 "Culfa G, Sarıoğlu E, Daşdemir M, Çelikten E, Kaya Nacarkahya T (2022) Investigation of fluid distribution and rewet performance with the use of different inner layer design and top sheets in sanitary napkins. Tekstil ve Mühendis 29:301–307.                    https://doi.org/10.7216/teksmuh.1222535                                    ")\].
  
  **Figure 5**

Polym Adv Technol 29:2853–2867")\]. Reprinted from the J Eng Fiber Fabr, 14, Liu H, Feng Y, Qian X, Preparation and properties of eccentric hollow fiber nonwovens for acquisition distribution layer, Copyright (2019), with permission from Sage Publishers.
  
  ### Absorbent layer
  
  The absorbent core is composed of a blend of cellulose fibres and superabsorbent polymer (SAP) granules encapsulated with cellulose or polypropylene. This layer functions as a storage layer for menstrual fluid, as the SAPs lock the fluid in with intermolecular interactions (see Fig. [6](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Fig6)), even under pressure \[ [30](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR30 "Bae J, Kwon H, Kim J (2018) Safety evaluation of absorbent hygiene pads: a review on assessment framework and test methods. Sustainability 10:4146.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/su10114146                                    ")\].
  
  **Figure 6**
  
  ![Figure 6](https://media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1007%2Fs10853-025-11151-7/MediaObjects/10853_2025_11151_Fig6_HTML.png)The alternative text for this image may have been generated using AI.
  
  [Full size image](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7/figures/6)
  
  The general interaction between fluid and sodium polyacrylate SAP found in the absorbent layer.
  
  Superabsorbent polymers (SAPs) are three-dimensional polymeric structures that are able to absorb water or other fluids from ten to several hundred times their own weight, without compromising their three-dimensional structure. This high swelling ability of SAPs is due to the electrostatic repulsion of similar charges of the functional groups in the network structure, causing the expansion of the network \[ [38](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR38 "Duquette D, Dumont MJ (2019) Comparative studies of chemical crosslinking reactions and applications of bio-based hydrogels. Polym Bull 76:2683–2710")\].
  
  SAPs are grouped according to the nature of the type of crosslinks holding the structure together, which can either be physical or chemical. Physical crosslinking occurs when hydrogen and ionic bonds are what holds the polymer network together.
  
  Chemical crosslinking occurs when covalent bonds, created either by graft copolymerization, photo-initiation, high-energy irradiation, microwave radiation, enzymatic crosslinking, and crosslinking agents, keeps the polymer chains together. Therefore, chemically crosslinked SAPs have superior stability. Furthermore, SAPs can also be classified as neutral, cationic, and anionic. Commercial SAPs are generally made from components that are non-biodegradable or that breakdown into toxic products. The greatest issues surrounding SAPs that are used commercially today are that it is made with petrochemical components, the production cost is high, toxicity of materials, and the negative environmental effects that its production causes \[ [39](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR39 "Fang S, Wang G, Xing R, Chen X, Liu S, Qin Y, Li K, Wang X, Li R, Li P (2019) Synthesis of superabsorbent polymers based on chitosan derivative graft acrylic acid-co-acrylamide and its property testing. Int J Biol Macromol 132:575–584.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ijbiomac.2019.03.176                                    ")\]. Therefore, research has explored synthesizing SAPs from biopolymers, since it is natural, biodegradable, and biocompatible. Figure [7](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Fig7) shows the type of materials that superabsorbent polymers can be made of \[ [40](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR40 "Chen J, Wu J, Raffa P, Picchioni F, Koning CE (2022) Superabsorbent polymers: from long-established, microplastics generating systems, to sustainable, biodegradable and future proof alternatives. Prog Polym Sci 125:101475.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.progpolymsci.2021.101475                                    ")\].
  
  **Figure 7**
  
  ![Figure 7](https://media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1007%2Fs10853-025-11151-7/MediaObjects/10853_2025_11151_Fig7_HTML.png)The alternative text for this image may have been generated using AI.
  
  [Full size image](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7/figures/7)
  
  Types of material used to make SAPs as illustrated by Chen et. al. \[ [30](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR30 "Bae J, Kwon H, Kim J (2018) Safety evaluation of absorbent hygiene pads: a review on assessment framework and test methods. Sustainability 10:4146.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/su10114146                                    ")\]. Reprinted from Progress in Polymer Science, 125, Chen J, Wu J, Raffa P, Picchioni F, Koning CE, Superabsorbent Polymers: From long-established, microplastics generating systems, to sustainable, biodegradable and future proof alternatives, Copyright (2022), with permission from Elsevier.
  
  SAPs are typically made from polymers with a high affinity for water such as polyvinyl alcohol (PVA), acrylamide, polyethylene oxide (PEO) \[ [33](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR33 "Kakonke G, Tesfaye T, Sithole B, Ntunka M (2019) Review on the manufacturing and properties of nonwoven superabsorbent core fabrics used in disposable diapers. Int J Chem Sci 17:302.                    https://doi.org/10.21767/0972-768X.1000302                                    ")\], and sodium polyacrylate that can absorb liquid hundreds of times its own weight \[ [41](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR41 "Gosden D, Studley M, Rossiter J (2023) Material extrusion of sodium polyacrylate superabsorbent polymer. Addit Manuf 78:103886.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.addma.2023.103886                                    ")\].
  
  Sodium polyacrylate (depicted in Fig. [8](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Fig8)) is a commonly used SAP in absorbent products \[ [32](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR32 "Dixit S, Sharma M (2023) Benign inflammatory vulval dermatoses: diagnostic approach and management strategies. Obstet Gynaecol Reprod Med 33:141–149.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ogrm.2023.02.004                                    ")\]. It is a partially neutralized polymer with incomplete crosslinking between the polymer chains. The fluid diffuses through the chain network via osmosis. It forms strong interactions with components of the fluid, such as the hydrogen bonds \[ [33](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR33 "Kakonke G, Tesfaye T, Sithole B, Ntunka M (2019) Review on the manufacturing and properties of nonwoven superabsorbent core fabrics used in disposable diapers. Int J Chem Sci 17:302.                    https://doi.org/10.21767/0972-768X.1000302                                    ")\]. The polymer will stop absorbing fluid once an equilibrium is reached, when the concentrations of water molecules and salts inside are the same as outside the SAP. Once this point is reached, the repulsion between ionic groups unwind the polymer chains creating a gel-like polymer. If there are more reactants (salts such as sodium) present in the fluid, the SAP will be less absorbing, as it counteracts the equilibrium effect \[ [33](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR33 "Kakonke G, Tesfaye T, Sithole B, Ntunka M (2019) Review on the manufacturing and properties of nonwoven superabsorbent core fabrics used in disposable diapers. Int J Chem Sci 17:302.                    https://doi.org/10.21767/0972-768X.1000302                                    "), [42](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR42 "Mas’ud Z, Khotib M, Farid M, Nur A, Amroni M (2013) Superabsorbent derived from cassava waste pulp. Int J Recycl Org Waste Agric 2:8.                    https://doi.org/10.1186/2251-7715-2-8                                    "), [43](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR43 "Qi Z, Hu X (2022) Water absorbency of super absorbent polymer based on flexible polymeric network. Eur Polym J 166:111045.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.eurpolymj.2022.111045                                    "), [44](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR44 "Zhao C, Zhang M, Liu Z, Guo Y, Zhang Q (2019) Salt-tolerant superabsorbent polymer with high capacity of water-nutrient retention derived from sulfamic acid-modified starch. ACS Omega 4:5923–5930.                    https://doi.org/10.1021/acsomega.9b00486                                    ")\]. Naturally, these SAPs are not soluble in water because of cross-linking; therefore, menstrual fluids will not dissolve it, also not allowing reflux back to the body \[ [30](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR30 "Bae J, Kwon H, Kim J (2018) Safety evaluation of absorbent hygiene pads: a review on assessment framework and test methods. Sustainability 10:4146.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/su10114146                                    ")\]. The properties for creating an ideal SAP are tabulated in Table [2](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Tab2) \[ [25](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR25 "Bashari A, Rouhani Shirvan A, Shakeri M (2018) Cellulose-based hydrogels for personal care products. Polym Adv Technol 29:2853–2867")\].

  [Full size image](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7/figures/8)
  
  The polymer structure of a polyacrylate SAP as illustrated by Dixit et. al. M + is usually a sodium ion and the X-R-X crosslinking agent is usually MBA \[ [22](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR22 "Ferguson KK, Rosen EM, Rosario Z, Feric Z, Calafat AM, McElrath TF, Vélez Vega C, Cordero JF, Alshawabkeh A, Meeker JD (2019) Environmental phthalate exposure and preterm birth in the PROTECT birth cohort. Environ Int 132:105099.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.envint.2019.105099                                    ")\].

  ### Back sheet
  
  The back sheet is the waterproof polyethylene or polypropylene layer beneath the absorbent core that keeps the menstrual fluid in the absorbent layer and prevents it from leaking into undergarments. In sanitary napkins, the back sheet has a thin layer of adhesive on it, to fixate it on undergarments. The back sheet and adhesive layer are reported to have negligible skin exposure through reflux \[ [30](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR30 "Bae J, Kwon H, Kim J (2018) Safety evaluation of absorbent hygiene pads: a review on assessment framework and test methods. Sustainability 10:4146.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/su10114146                                    ")\].
  
  Peberdy evaluated the awareness of the environmental impact of menstrual products by users \[ [45](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR45 "Peberdy E, Jones A, Green D (2019) A study into public awareness of the environmental impact of menstrual products and product choice. Sustainability 11:473.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/su11020473                                    ")\]. Through surveys, they found that non-organic disposable sanitary pads and non-organic disposable tampons are the most preferred menstrual products. They remain among the most preferred products amid participants who are fully aware of the presence of plastic and the possible environmental impact of these pads \[ [45](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR45 "Peberdy E, Jones A, Green D (2019) A study into public awareness of the environmental impact of menstrual products and product choice. Sustainability 11:473.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/su11020473                                    ")\]. They attributed its popularity to low cost and availability. Additionally, they found that most of the participants were open to the idea that plastic containing disposable menstrual products need to adapt to the changing world, with alternatives that have a lesser impact on the environment. Their research shows that there is a correlation between awareness and choice of menstrual product \[ [45](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR45 "Peberdy E, Jones A, Green D (2019) A study into public awareness of the environmental impact of menstrual products and product choice. Sustainability 11:473.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/su11020473                                    ")\].
  
  ### Adhesive
  
  Adhesive can be categorized into four different categories: water-based, solvent-based, hot-melt, and reactive adhesives (see Table [3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Tab3)) \[ [46](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR46 "Williams JD, Frowen KE, Nixon RL (2007) Allergic contact dermatitis from methyldibromo glutaronitrile in a sanitary pad and review of Australian clinic data. Contact Dermatitis 56:164–167.                    https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1600-0536.2007.01040.x                                    ")\]. The choice of adhesive includes the bonding strength required, the substance to be adhered, and the rate of adhesive drying. Hot-melt adhesives are applied to nappies and disposable sanitary pads during manufacturing with machines that can produce up to 750 items per minute. Therefore, an adhesive with rapid drying abilities is required \[ [46](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR46 "Williams JD, Frowen KE, Nixon RL (2007) Allergic contact dermatitis from methyldibromo glutaronitrile in a sanitary pad and review of Australian clinic data. Contact Dermatitis 56:164–167.                    https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1600-0536.2007.01040.x                                    ")\]. Hot-melt adhesives (HMA) are thermoplastics that are solid at room temperature and applied to the product in their molten state. These adhesives do not require a solvent such as water or volatile organic solvents. Currently, most HMAs are made from petroleum sources such as polyolefins, polyurethanes, polyamides, polyesters, butadiene, isoprene, styrene, and ethyl vinyl acetate \[ [47](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR47 "Gadhave RVI (2022) Starch grafted water resistant polyvinyl acetate-based wood adhesive: a review. Open J Org Polym Mater 12:17–30.                    https://doi.org/10.4236/ojopm.2022.122002                                    ")\].
  
  **Table 3 Different categories of adhesives and some of their characteristics \[ [46](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR46 "Williams JD, Frowen KE, Nixon RL (2007) Allergic contact dermatitis from methyldibromo glutaronitrile in a sanitary pad and review of Australian clinic data. Contact Dermatitis 56:164–167.                    https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1600-0536.2007.01040.x                                    ")\]**

  ## Standards and specifications for disposable sanitary pads
  
  ### Country specific standards
  
  Different countries or regions have various regulations and standards that sanitary pads must meet. Table [4](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Tab4) displays the standards for disposable sanitary pads in different countries/regions. The majority of these regulations make it optional for manufacturers to disclose the chemicals and materials used in the disposable sanitary pads. This creates a loophole to hide possible harmful chemicals from consumers.
  
  **Table 4 Standards for the manufacturing of disposable sanitary pads in 15 different countries/regions \[ [49](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR49 "VIHAR V (2021) Development and compliance of quality standards for disposable and reusable menstrual health pads in LMICs. In: Reproductive Health Supplies Coalition.                    https://www.rhsupplies.org/fileadmin/uploads/rhsc/Uploads/Other/Standards_for_Menstrual_Products_-_Summary_Report.pdf                                    . Accessed 18 Feb 2025")\]**
  
  [Full size table](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7/tables/4)
  
  Menstrual equity (ME) refers to the right to menstrual dignity \[ [48](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR48 "Scranton A (2022) What’s in your period product? An investigation of ingredients disclosed on product labels. In: Women’s Voices for the Earth.                    www.womensvoices.orgorcall                                    . Accessed 18 Feb 2025")\]. This includes having access to menstrual hygiene products, regardless of living circumstances, not paying ‘luxury’ taxes on products, and having the right to know what chemicals are in products that may affect the user’s health. This movement has had success in many countries by alleviating taxes on menstrual hygiene products and in the year 2019 New York became the first state in the USA to require manufacturers to disclose all the ingredients in their sanitary products \[ [48](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR48 "Scranton A (2022) What’s in your period product? An investigation of ingredients disclosed on product labels. In: Women’s Voices for the Earth.                    www.womensvoices.orgorcall                                    . Accessed 18 Feb 2025")\].
  
  In 2022, Scranton published a study identifying the ingredients listed by manufacturers on the packaging of over 140 sanitary products in seven states \[ [48](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR48 "Scranton A (2022) What’s in your period product? An investigation of ingredients disclosed on product labels. In: Women’s Voices for the Earth.                    www.womensvoices.orgorcall                                    . Accessed 18 Feb 2025")\]. The study found that some brands describe their ingredients in vague terms such as “adhesive” or “white pigment” instead of the specific chemical name. Other brands list their ingredient with specific chemical names and functions. In the same study, more than 62 different ingredients/chemicals were identified that are used in the production of disposable sanitary pads in the USA, and over 38 ingredients/chemicals used by manufacturers to produce tampons. This shows that the chemical exposures and potential health risks from sanitary products are more complicated than manufacturers initially thought. Disclosing such information help users to identify ingredients that previously caused adverse effects, allows healthcare professionals to identify problematic ingredients and suggest better alternatives, and drives the search for safer replacements of hazardous chemicals \[ [48](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR48 "Scranton A (2022) What’s in your period product? An investigation of ingredients disclosed on product labels. In: Women’s Voices for the Earth.                    www.womensvoices.orgorcall                                    . Accessed 18 Feb 2025")\].
  
  ## Challenges associated with absorbent hygiene products
  
  Disposable sanitary pads have greatly improved women’s quality of life; however, it has also brought to light several issues. As previously mentioned, these AHPs are in constant contact with sensitive genital skin, which can lead to dermatitis, infections, and rashes. These are the primary issues users face. Secondary issues arise from long term exposure to the allergens and chemicals in the products. Not only do AHPs impact the health of their users, but their after-use impact is also concerning. Furthermore, the disposal practices of these products have dire effects on the environment, from the leaching of chemicals to plastic contamination. Soil, plants, animals, and water are all negatively affected by these products. The economic cost of these products on MHM is high especially in the Global South and might be countered by creating these products from locally available material. Lastly, in the Global South, many of the products are financially inaccessible to the majority of women, and their cultures and living conditions do not agree with the use of these products.
  
  ### The health and wellbeing of women using disposable sanitary pads
  
  Although most women know how to hygienically manage their menstruation, research shows that approximately 4.5 million American women suffer from menstrual health issues \[ [51](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR51 "Critchley HOD, Babayev E, Bulun SE, Clark S, Garcia-Grau I, Gregersen PK, Kilcoyne A, Kim J-YJ, Lavender M, Marsh EE, Matteson KA, Maybin JA, Metz CN, Moreno I, Silk K, Sommer M, Simon C, Tariyal R, Taylor HS, Wagner GP, Griffith LG (2020) Menstruation: science and society. Am J Obstet Gynecol 223:624–664.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ajog.2020.06.004                                    ")\]. Whether it is culture, socioeconomic status, or individual health that influences MHM, it is overlooked due to the seemingly readily availability of sanitary products and bathroom facilities. Particularly, concerning is the impact of commercial pads on women’s reproductive and general health. In America, menstrual products are classified as medical devices \[ [3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR3 "Hand J, Hwang C, Vogel W, Lopez C, Hwang S (2023) An exploration of market organic sanitary products for improving menstrual health and environmental impact. J Water Sanit Hyg Dev 13:63–77.                    https://doi.org/10.2166/washdev.2023.020                                    ")\] meaning that the ingredients of these products do not have to be disclosed to the consumer. Sanitary products have improved from the past to be more absorbent and flexible for comfort. However, the question remains whether the ingredients used to make these improvements are safe for humans and the environment. The reproductive health of women depends on the health of the vaginal skin, reproductive organs, and on the microbiota of the genital tract. This microbial community is dominated by the _Lactobacillus_ species which are responsible for the production of lactic acid, hydrogen peroxide, bio surfactants, and other substances that create a harsh environment for pathogenic bacteria. However, the stability of the genital tract’s microbiome depends on various factors, including the menstrual cycle, introduction of bacteria from the skin, the external environment (such as sexual contact, personal hygiene and medical treatments), oestrogen and progesterone levels, pH, and cervical mucus production \[ [52](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR52 "Chaban B, Links MG, Jayaprakash TP, Wagner EC, Bourque DK, Lohn Z, Albert AY, van Schalkwyk J, Reid G, Hemmingsen SM, Hill JE, Money DM (2014) Characterization of the vaginal microbiota of healthy Canadian women through the menstrual cycle. Microbiome 2:23.                    https://doi.org/10.1186/2049-2618-2-23                                    "), [53](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR53 "Linhares IM, Summers PR, Larsen B, Giraldo PC, Witkin SS (2011) Contemporary perspectives on vaginal pH and lactobacilli. Am J Obstet Gynecol 204:120.e1-120.e5.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ajog.2010.07.010                                    "), [54](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR54 "Günther V, Allahqoli L, Watrowski R, Maass N, Ackermann J, von Otte S, Alkatout I (2022) Vaginal Microbiome in Reproductive Medicine. Diagnostics 12:1948.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/diagnostics12081948                                    "), [55](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR55 "Ratten LK, Plummer EL, Bradshaw CS, Fairley CK, Murray GL, Garland SM, Bateson D, Tachedjian G, Masson L, Vodstrcil LA (2021) The effect of exogenous sex steroids on the vaginal microbiota: a systematic review. Front Cell Infect Microbiol 11:732423.                    https://doi.org/10.3389/fcimb.2021.732423                                    "), [56](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR56 "Kindinger LM, Bennett PR, Lee YS, Marchesi JR, Smith A, Cacciatore S, Holmes E, Nicholson JK, Teoh TG, MacIntyre DA (2017) The interaction between vaginal microbiota, cervical length, and vaginal progesterone treatment for preterm birth risk. Microbiome 5:6.                    https://doi.org/10.1186/s40168-016-0223-9                                    ")\].
  
  In the superabsorbent layer of disposable sanitary pads, synthetic fibres, such as viscose rayon, are used in conjunction with cotton to improve the absorbency of the product \[ [57](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR57 "Tierno PM, Hanna BA (1989) Ecology of toxic shock syndrome: amplification of toxic shock syndrome toxin 1 by materials of medical interest. Rev Infect Dis 11:S182–S187")\]. Viscose rayon is often classified as a natural polymer since it is made from wood pulp; however, it is a synthetic fibre as it is made by exposing wood pulp to carbon disulphide. The use of synthetic fibres in pads and tampons has been correlated with the increase of menstrual toxic shock syndrome (MTSS) cases. Tierno _et. al._ analysed the mechanisms of toxic shock syndrome toxin production with different fibres \[ [57](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR57 "Tierno PM, Hanna BA (1989) Ecology of toxic shock syndrome: amplification of toxic shock syndrome toxin 1 by materials of medical interest. Rev Infect Dis 11:S182–S187")\].
  
  Materials that have greater absorbency than cotton, i.e. synthetic fibres absorb and concentrate proteins between fibres, creating an ideal environment for the production of the toxic shock syndrome toxin. The materials that caused the greatest production of the MTSS toxin (in decreasing order) are polyester, carboxymethyl cellulose, polyacrylates, viscose rayon, gelatine foam, polyurethane, and cotton \[ [57](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR57 "Tierno PM, Hanna BA (1989) Ecology of toxic shock syndrome: amplification of toxic shock syndrome toxin 1 by materials of medical interest. Rev Infect Dis 11:S182–S187")\]. MTSS is caused by the super antigenic exotoxin released by the bacteria _Staphylococcus aureus_ ( _S. aureus_), which is normally present in the vagina. However, synthetic fibres provide a better environment for this bacteria’s rapid growth, releasing this toxin into the body, affecting the nervous system, digestive system, muscular system, and the reproductive system \[ [3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR3 "Hand J, Hwang C, Vogel W, Lopez C, Hwang S (2023) An exploration of market organic sanitary products for improving menstrual health and environmental impact. J Water Sanit Hyg Dev 13:63–77.                    https://doi.org/10.2166/washdev.2023.020                                    "), [58](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR58 "Nonfoux L, Chiaruzzi M, Badiou C, Baude J, Tristan A, Thioulouse J, Muller D, Prigent-Combaret C, Lina G (2018) Impact of currently marketed tampons and menstrual cups on Staphylococcus aureus growth and toxic shock syndrome toxin 1 production in vitro. Appl Environ Microbiol 84:00351–00418.                    https://doi.org/10.1128/AEM.00351-18                                    ")\].
  
  Disposable sanitary pads can also cause rashes in the genital area \[ [59](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR59 "Fletcher J (2023) What causes pad rash, and what does it look like? In: Medical News Today.                    https://www.medicalnewstoday.com/articles/pad-rash                                    . Accessed 18 Feb 2025")\]. These rashes are caused by friction, allergic reactions to materials in a pad, and irritation from moisture and heat. Movement of the sanitary pad in a woman’s undergarments can contribute to a friction rash on the vulva. An allergic skin reaction caused by the materials and chemicals used in disposable sanitary pads is termed ‘contact dermatitis’ by healthcare professionals. Several materials, such as fragrances, adhesives, and superabsorbent polymers, can cause contact dermatitis. Heat and moisture build-up in sanitary pads are influenced by the its ability to absorb menstrual fluid. If the sanitary pad cannot absorb moisture efficiently to create a cooler and drier environment between the vulvar skin and the top sheet of the sanitary pad, bacterial growth is promoted. Bacterial growth can cause unpleasant odours, irritation, lower reproductive tract infections, bacterial vaginosis, and yeast infections \[ [59](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR59 "Fletcher J (2023) What causes pad rash, and what does it look like? In: Medical News Today.                    https://www.medicalnewstoday.com/articles/pad-rash                                    . Accessed 18 Feb 2025")\].
  
  Scented sanitary products are popular since women are very self-conscious during menses. However, there are many components in fragrances that may cause allergic reactions, especially on sensitive skin. A case was reported about a woman that suffered from vulvar dermatitis while using deodorized sanitary products \[ [60](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR60 "Larsen WG (1979) Sanitary napkin dermatitis due to the perfume. Arch Dermatol 115:363.                    https://doi.org/10.1001/archderm.1979.04010030065027                                    ")\]. The perfume used by the manufacturer (perfume content of the sanitary pad was 5 mg per pad) contained cinnamaldehyde and cinnamic alcohol. These two components, among others commonly used in perfumes (see Table [5](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Tab5)), are the cause of most allergies \[ [60](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR60 "Larsen WG (1979) Sanitary napkin dermatitis due to the perfume. Arch Dermatol 115:363.                    https://doi.org/10.1001/archderm.1979.04010030065027                                    ")\].
  
  **Table 5 The 24 most common fragrance components that can cause allergic reactions \[ [16](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR16 "Desmedt B, Marcelis Q, Zhilivoda D, Deconinck E (2020) Sensitizing fragrances in absorbent hygiene products. Contact Dermatitis 82:279–282.                    https://doi.org/10.1111/cod.13472                                    ")\]**
  
  [Full size table](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7/tables/5)
  
  Fragrances are frequently used in products to mask unpleasant odours or attract consumers to the product \[ [16](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR16 "Desmedt B, Marcelis Q, Zhilivoda D, Deconinck E (2020) Sensitizing fragrances in absorbent hygiene products. Contact Dermatitis 82:279–282.                    https://doi.org/10.1111/cod.13472                                    ")\]. However, these fragrances are not regulated to the same extent in all industries. In Europe, the cosmetic and toy industry has specific regulations that restrict the use of certain fragrances and mandate the communication of their presence. However, general consumer products, such as sanitary products, are governed by different safety regulations, and thus, fragrances are not as strongly regulated in sanitary products in Europe. In the USA and Korea, sanitary products are classified as medical devices and _quasi_-drugs, respectively, requiring them to be subjected to in-depth safety evaluations but not listing ingredients \[ [16](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR16 "Desmedt B, Marcelis Q, Zhilivoda D, Deconinck E (2020) Sensitizing fragrances in absorbent hygiene products. Contact Dermatitis 82:279–282.                    https://doi.org/10.1111/cod.13472                                    ")\] (Fig. [9](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Fig9)).
  
  **Figure 9**
  
  ![Figure 9](https://media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1007%2Fs10853-025-11151-7/MediaObjects/10853_2025_11151_Fig9_HTML.png)The alternative text for this image may have been generated using AI.
  
  [Full size image](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7/figures/9)
  
  Impact of disposable sanitary products on the health and wellbeing of women.
  
  The presence of fragrances was examined in sanitary products that vaguely communicated the presence of perfumes using words such as “fresh” on their packaging. The results showed the presence of five allergenic components of fragrances: limonene, linalool, citronellal, geraniol, and hydroxycitronella \[ [16](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR16 "Desmedt B, Marcelis Q, Zhilivoda D, Deconinck E (2020) Sensitizing fragrances in absorbent hygiene products. Contact Dermatitis 82:279–282.                    https://doi.org/10.1111/cod.13472                                    ")\]. Figure [10](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Fig10) shows the abiotic and biotic activation of geraniol to form its sensitizers (molecules in red) \[ [61](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR61 "Bråred Christensson J, Hagvall L, Karlberg A-T (2016) Fragrance allergens, overview with a focus on recent developments and understanding of abiotic and biotic activation. Cosmetics 3:19.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/cosmetics3020019                                    ")\]. In one product, two extra components were detected: hexyl cinnamal and benzyl salicylate, with concentrations of more than 30 µg/g. According to the cosmetic and toy regulations of Europe, fragrance components present in products that fall under these regulations, with concentrations of more than 10 µg/g, must be labelled. Sanitary product manufacturers do not report the presence of these fragrance components, primarily because consumers are not aware that fragrances are in the products, and exposure to the fragrances in the genital area is lower than in the area used for patch testing. Furthermore, they are not obligated to report them since the general product safety regulations do not require them to do so \[ [16](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR16 "Desmedt B, Marcelis Q, Zhilivoda D, Deconinck E (2020) Sensitizing fragrances in absorbent hygiene products. Contact Dermatitis 82:279–282.                    https://doi.org/10.1111/cod.13472                                    ")\].
  
  **Figure 10**
  
  ![Figure 10](https://media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1007%2Fs10853-025-11151-7/MediaObjects/10853_2025_11151_Fig10_HTML.png)The alternative text for this image may have been generated using AI.
  
  [Full size image](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7/figures/10)
  
  The activation of geraniol with air (abiotic) and the enzyme CYP 450 (biotic) to form various sensitizers. Strong sensitizers are depicted in red, where the rest are moderate sensitizers \[ [61](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR61 "Bråred Christensson J, Hagvall L, Karlberg A-T (2016) Fragrance allergens, overview with a focus on recent developments and understanding of abiotic and biotic activation. Cosmetics 3:19.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/cosmetics3020019                                    ")\].
  
  Many sanitary pads contain SAPs as it allows for a thin design, a drier, and more comfortable fit. A common SAP used in sanitary pads is sodium polyacrylate where monomer acrylic components are polymerized to form polyacrylates, which are said to be safe and unable to cause allergic reactions. However, the monomers can be strong irritants or allergens. A woman was diagnosed with a contact dermatitis, which began when she used a pantiliner with a fibrous absorbent layer containing a polyacrylate SAP. She was found to be allergic to most acrylates tested on her. The liner caused her contact dermatitis as it contained unreacted acrylates from the polyacrylate in the liner. During the synthesis of polyacrylates, not all the monomers may react, and can get trapped in the polymer lattice. Therefore, four mechanisms of acrylate exposure are suggested: incomplete polymerization of polyacrylates resulting in exposure to uncured acrylates, trace monomers in the final product, the release of monomer components during shearing forces of the sanitary products, and movement of acrylates into solution \[ [62](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR62 "Sauder MB, Pratt MD (2015) Acrylate systemic contact dermatitis. Dermatitis 26:235–238.                    https://doi.org/10.1097/DER.0000000000000136                                    ")\].
  
  Colophonium, referred to as rosin, tall oil, or abietic oil, is obtained from trees from the Pinaceae family. The major constituents that make up colophonium are shown in Fig. [11](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Fig11) \[ [63](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR63 "Coll R, Udas S, Jacoby WA (2001) Conversion of the rosin acid fraction of crude tall oil into fuels and chemicals. Energy Fuels 15:1166–1172.                    https://doi.org/10.1021/ef010018a                                    ")\]. Colophonium is obtained from tree gum by tapping it from living trees and distilling it, or from wood by pulping it \[ [64](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR64 "Rademaker M (2004) Allergic contact dermatitis to a sanitary pad. Australas J Dermatol 45:234–235.                    https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1440-0960.2004.00105.x                                    ")\]. There are three reported cases of contact dermatitis where wood pulp containing colophonium was used in sanitary pads \[ [64](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR64 "Rademaker M (2004) Allergic contact dermatitis to a sanitary pad. Australas J Dermatol 45:234–235.                    https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1440-0960.2004.00105.x                                    "), [65](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR65 "Salzano G, Galletta F, Caminiti L, Lonia P, Donia V, Pajno GB, Passanisi S, Lombardo F (2021) Vulvar contact dermatitis caused by sensitization to colophonium in a patient with type 1 diabetes. Contact Dermatitis 85:364–366.                    https://doi.org/10.1111/cod.13851                                    "), [66](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR66 "Wujanto L, Wakelin S (2012) Allergic contact dermatitis to colophonium in a sanitary pad-an overlooked allergen? Contact Dermatitis 66:161–162.                    https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1600-0536.2011.02006.x                                    ")\]. According to the European Union legislation (EC No. 1272/2008) any product containing more than 1% colophonium/rosin, should be labelled as follows: “H317: May cause an allergic skin reaction” \[ [65](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR65 "Salzano G, Galletta F, Caminiti L, Lonia P, Donia V, Pajno GB, Passanisi S, Lombardo F (2021) Vulvar contact dermatitis caused by sensitization to colophonium in a patient with type 1 diabetes. Contact Dermatitis 85:364–366.                    https://doi.org/10.1111/cod.13851                                    ")\]. Kanerva _et. al._ studied the presence of colophonium in unlabelled pads from Finland and Denmark, which included very popular pad brands. They found that the levels of colophonium in the disposable sanitary pads are similar to those found in mascaras. Therefore, the levels can be enough to cause an allergic reaction in sensitized individuals \[ [67](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR67 "Kanerva L, Rintala H, Henriks-Eckerman K, Engström K (2001) Colophonium in sanitary pads. Contact Dermatitis 44:53–54.                    https://doi.org/10.1034/j.1600-0536.2001.440107-27.x                                    ")\].
  
  **Figure 11**
  
  ![Figure 11](https://media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1007%2Fs10853-025-11151-7/MediaObjects/10853_2025_11151_Fig11_HTML.png)The alternative text for this image may have been generated using AI.
  
  [Full size image](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7/figures/11)
  
  Major constituents in colophonium \[ [63](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR63 "Coll R, Udas S, Jacoby WA (2001) Conversion of the rosin acid fraction of crude tall oil into fuels and chemicals. Energy Fuels 15:1166–1172.                    https://doi.org/10.1021/ef010018a                                    ")\].
  
  As previously mentioned, hot-melt glues are used to attach the different layers of a sanitary pad together. These glues contain volatile compounds that can be released with heat and also cause adverse effects. Once such incident was reported when a woman developed a rash upon wearing a brand of disposable sanitary pads claiming to be ‘environmentally friendly’ \[ [46](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR46 "Williams JD, Frowen KE, Nixon RL (2007) Allergic contact dermatitis from methyldibromo glutaronitrile in a sanitary pad and review of Australian clinic data. Contact Dermatitis 56:164–167.                    https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1600-0536.2007.01040.x                                    ")\]. The patch testing revealed she was allergic to a biocide, methyldibromo glutaronitrile (MDBGN), used in the holt-melt adhesive applied to the sanitary pad. MDBGN is used as a preservative in many products in Europe; however, was replaced with Euxyl K400 (see Fig. [12](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Fig12)), when an increased incidence of allergic contact dermatitis was reported. In Australia, chloro- and methyl isothiazolinones are the biocides of choice; however, industry is limited to use up to 15 ppm of this biocide, or indicate the risk of sensitization on the package of the product \[ [46](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR46 "Williams JD, Frowen KE, Nixon RL (2007) Allergic contact dermatitis from methyldibromo glutaronitrile in a sanitary pad and review of Australian clinic data. Contact Dermatitis 56:164–167.                    https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1600-0536.2007.01040.x                                    ")\].
  
  **Figure 12**
  
  ![Figure 12](https://media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1007%2Fs10853-025-11151-7/MediaObjects/10853_2025_11151_Fig12_HTML.png)The alternative text for this image may have been generated using AI.
  
  [Full size image](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7/figures/12)
  
  The structures of the biocides used in adhesives \[ [36](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR36 "Venkataraman D, Shabani E, Park JH (2023) Advancement of nonwoven fabrics in personal protective equipment. Materials 16:3964.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/ma16113964                                    ")\].
  
  Different sanitary pad brands prefer the use of different types of fibres in the top sheet and absorbent layer, especially with the development of new “organic”, “environmentally friendly”, and “biodegradable” sanitary pads. However, one needs to consider what effect these different fibres will have on the health of the genital tract. Therefore, a study was done on the effect of the type of fibres used in disposable sanitary pads on the vaginal microecology of 1002 women in Beijing \[ [68](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR68 "Sun Y, Li C, Yan Y, Lai A, Peng X, Yue X, Li Y, Liu J, Liu Y (2022) Effects of hemp sanitary pads on the vaginal microecology.
