Comput Math Methods Med 2022:1–6.                    https://doi.org/10.1155/2022/4435722                                    ")\]. During menstruation, oestrogen levels fluctuate, causing the normal vaginal pH of 3.8–4.5 to fluctuate as well. Consequently, the risk of infections increase as the vaginal resistance to pathogenic microorganisms are dependent on the acidic pH. It was formulated that since hemp fibres have antibiotic properties, these fibres will improve the microecosystem of the vagina and reduce the risk of infection after menstruation. The results confirmed the hypothesis, and showed improved vaginal microecosystems in women that used hemp disposable sanitary pads when compared to women who used cotton disposable sanitary pads. They credited these results to the unique porous structure of the hemp fibre. There is an abundance of oxygen in the pores of the hemp fibre, inhibiting the growth of anaerobic bacteria. These fibres also contain organic salts and active phenols that also have the ability to limit the survival of various bacteria. Even though the research is not enough to reflect what will happen in a population of people, it shows how important it is to understand the menstrual cycle and to choose appropriate menstrual hygiene products \[ [68](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR68 "Sun Y, Li C, Yan Y, Lai A, Peng X, Yue X, Li Y, Liu J, Liu Y (2022) Effects of hemp sanitary pads on the vaginal microecology. Comput Math Methods Med 2022:1–6.                    https://doi.org/10.1155/2022/4435722                                    ")\].
  
  Studies have been conducted to determine whether some common plasticizers, volatile organic compounds, dioxins, furans, and synthetic fibres found in disposable sanitary pads can be linked to endometriosis, cancer, reproductive organ damage, and pregnancy complications \[ [3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR3 "Hand J, Hwang C, Vogel W, Lopez C, Hwang S (2023) An exploration of market organic sanitary products for improving menstrual health and environmental impact. J Water Sanit Hyg Dev 13:63–77.                    https://doi.org/10.2166/washdev.2023.020                                    "), [21](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR21 "Weuve J, Hauser R, Calafat AM, Missmer SA, Wise LA (2010) Association of exposure to phthalates with endometriosis and uterine leiomyomata: findings from NHANES, 1999–2004. Environ Health Perspect 118:825–832.                    https://doi.org/10.1289/ehp.0901543                                    "), [22](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR22 "Ferguson KK, Rosen EM, Rosario Z, Feric Z, Calafat AM, McElrath TF, Vélez Vega C, Cordero JF, Alshawabkeh A, Meeker JD (2019) Environmental phthalate exposure and preterm birth in the PROTECT birth cohort. Environ Int 132:105099.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.envint.2019.105099                                    "), [23](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR23 "Kim HY, Lee JD, Kim J-Y, Lee JY, Bae O-N, Choi Y-K, Baek E, Kang S, Min C, Seo K, Choi K, Lee B-M, Kim K-B (2019) Risk assessment of volatile organic compounds (VOCs) detected in sanitary pads. J Toxicol Environ Health A 82:678–695.                    https://doi.org/10.1080/15287394.2019.1642607                                    ")\]. Sanitary products contain polyethylene and polypropylene, which contain plasticizers, such as phthalates to improve pliability. Phthalates may damage fertility, foetuses, and are toxic to aquatic life \[ [3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR3 "Hand J, Hwang C, Vogel W, Lopez C, Hwang S (2023) An exploration of market organic sanitary products for improving menstrual health and environmental impact. J Water Sanit Hyg Dev 13:63–77.                    https://doi.org/10.2166/washdev.2023.020                                    ")\]. Phthalate exposure has also been associated with premature puberty \[ [69](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR69 "Kim YJ, Kwon A, Jung MK, Kim KE, Suh J, Chae HW, Kim DH, Ha S, Seo GH, Kim H-S (2019) Incidence and prevalence of central precocious puberty in Korea: an epidemiologic study based on a national database. J Pediatr 208:221–228.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jpeds.2018.12.022                                    ")\], preterm births \[ [22](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR22 "Ferguson KK, Rosen EM, Rosario Z, Feric Z, Calafat AM, McElrath TF, Vélez Vega C, Cordero JF, Alshawabkeh A, Meeker JD (2019) Environmental phthalate exposure and preterm birth in the PROTECT birth cohort. Environ Int 132:105099.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.envint.2019.105099                                    ")\], and ovulation disorders \[ [21](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR21 "Weuve J, Hauser R, Calafat AM, Missmer SA, Wise LA (2010) Association of exposure to phthalates with endometriosis and uterine leiomyomata: findings from NHANES, 1999–2004. Environ Health Perspect 118:825–832.                    https://doi.org/10.1289/ehp.0901543                                    ")\], Volatile organic compounds (VOC) such as chloromethane, chloroform, benzene, 1,4-dioxane, and styrene are also present in these products. It is typically are released from adhesives, binders, and fragrances in disposable sanitary pads. It is concerning that these VOCs are present in a product that comes into direct contact with sensitive skin, as most of them are irritants, carcinogens, and can cause damage to organs with prolonged exposure \[ [3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR3 "Hand J, Hwang C, Vogel W, Lopez C, Hwang S (2023) An exploration of market organic sanitary products for improving menstrual health and environmental impact. J Water Sanit Hyg Dev 13:63–77.                    https://doi.org/10.2166/washdev.2023.020                                    ")\]. Dioxins and furans are present in sanitary products that are chlorine bleached. These chemicals have been associated with causing endometriosis, cancer in female reproductive organs, and abnormal growth of cells around the uterus muscle \[ [18](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR18 "Shin JH, Ahn YG (2007) Analysis of polychlorinated dibenzo-p-dioxins and dibenzo-furans in sanitary products of women. Text Res J 77:597–603.                    https://doi.org/10.1177/0040517507078786                                    ")\]. The FDA reports the adverse effects of dioxins in tampons as ‘negligible’. However, even though no studies have been published on the in vivo diffusion of dioxins, it is known to bioaccumulate with a cumulative effect \[ [3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR3 "Hand J, Hwang C, Vogel W, Lopez C, Hwang S (2023) An exploration of market organic sanitary products for improving menstrual health and environmental impact. J Water Sanit Hyg Dev 13:63–77.                    https://doi.org/10.2166/washdev.2023.020                                    ")\].
  
  The back sheet of a pad is generally made from polyethylene (PE) or polypropylene (PPE) plastic; however, the top layer of a pad and the cover of a tampon is often made from it too. Other components of sanitary care products that also contain PE, PPE, and polyester are the removal string of tampons, the tampon applicator, the wrappers, and the product’s packaging. Figure [3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Fig3) shows the total plastic content of ultra-thin disposable sanitary pads are 36% \[ [25](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR25 "Bashari A, Rouhani Shirvan A, Shakeri M (2018) Cellulose-based hydrogels for personal care products. Polym Adv Technol 29:2853–2867")\]. These plastic polymers, including the hot-melt adhesive, contain phthalates to enhance the flexibility of the plastic used for the back sheet and ductility and gel properties of the hot-melt glue \[ [70](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR70 "Gao CJ, Wang F, Shen HM, Kannan K, Guo Y (2020) Feminine hygiene products - a neglected source of phthalate exposure in women. Environ Sci Technol 54:930–937.                    https://doi.org/10.1021/acs.est.9b03927                                    "), [71](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR71 "Tang Z, Chai M, Cheng J, Wang Y, Huang Q (2019) Occurrence and distribution of phthalates in sanitary napkins from six countries: implications for women’s health. Environ Sci Technol 53:13919–13928.                    https://doi.org/10.1021/acs.est.9b03838                                    ")\]. The high temperatures used during the production of disposable sanitary pads release these phthalates from the matrix. Since the skin of the vulva has a higher absorption efficiency than normal skin and the vaginal mucosa has an abundance of blood vessels, phthalates and other chemicals are absorbed directly into the bloodstream \[ [70](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR70 "Gao CJ, Wang F, Shen HM, Kannan K, Guo Y (2020) Feminine hygiene products - a neglected source of phthalate exposure in women. Environ Sci Technol 54:930–937.                    https://doi.org/10.1021/acs.est.9b03927                                    ")\].
  
  Phthalates have been reported to be associated with female genital tumours, to induce ovulation disorders, interfere with the secretion of ovarian hormones, interfere with ovarian endocrine functions, and affect embryonic development. This toxic compound is also found in other feminine care products (perfumes, deodorants, cosmetics, and hair care products), and other feminine hygiene products (bacterial creams and medical lotions) \[ [70](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR70 "Gao CJ, Wang F, Shen HM, Kannan K, Guo Y (2020) Feminine hygiene products - a neglected source of phthalate exposure in women. Environ Sci Technol 54:930–937.                    https://doi.org/10.1021/acs.est.9b03927                                    ")\] as it is used as a humectant, emollient, and enhances skin penetration of these products \[ [72](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR72 "Gao C-J, Kannan K (2020) Phthalates, bisphenols, parabens, and triclocarban in feminine hygiene products from the United States and their implications for human exposure. Environ Int 136:105465.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.envint.2020.105465                                    ")\]. Park _et. al._ proved that phthalates are also present in commercial disposable diapers, posing a risk not only to women who use disposable sanitary pads, but also to children who are exposed to these products \[ [19](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR19 "Park CJ, Barakat R, Ulanov A, Li Z, Lin PC, Chiu K, Zhou S, Perez P, Lee J, Flaws J, Ko CMJ (2019) Sanitary pads and diapers contain higher phthalate contents than those in common commercial plastic products. Reprod Toxicol 84:114–121.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.reprotox.2019.01.005                                    ")\]. Phthalates are not only carcinogenic and toxic to humans, but to the environment and other organisms as well. In addition to phthalates, parabens (an antimicrobial agent), and triclocarbons (antibacterial agent) are also present in disposable sanitary pads \[ [72](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR72 "Gao C-J, Kannan K (2020) Phthalates, bisphenols, parabens, and triclocarban in feminine hygiene products from the United States and their implications for human exposure. Environ Int 136:105465.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.envint.2020.105465                                    ")\].
  
  ### Environmental impact of absorbent hygiene products
  
  Polymers make up materials, such as plastics, that are ideal for many industrial products and consumer products \[ [73](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR73 "Narancic T, Verstichel S, Reddy Chaganti S, Morales-Gamez L, Kenny ST, De Wilde B, Babu Padamati R, O’Connor KE (2018) Biodegradable plastic blends create new possibilities for end-of-life management of plastics but they are not a panacea for plastic pollution. Environ Sci Technol 52:10441–10452.                    https://doi.org/10.1021/acs.est.8b02963                                    ")\]. Plastics are light weight, durable, inert, strong, and have low production costs; however, its complex 3D structure, high molecular weight, and hydrophobicity hinder its ability to degrade, causing it to accumulate in the environment \[ [74](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR74 "González-Pleiter M, Tamayo-Belda M, Pulido-Reyes G, Amariei G, Leganés F, Rosal R, Fernández-Piñas F (2019) Secondary nanoplastics released from a biodegradable microplastic severely impact freshwater environments. Environ Sci Nano 6:1382–1392.                    https://doi.org/10.1039/C8EN01427B                                    ")\]. Despite the many advantages plastics have to offer in the packaging, automotive, construction, sport, medical, and household sectors \[ [74](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR74 "González-Pleiter M, Tamayo-Belda M, Pulido-Reyes G, Amariei G, Leganés F, Rosal R, Fernández-Piñas F (2019) Secondary nanoplastics released from a biodegradable microplastic severely impact freshwater environments. Environ Sci Nano 6:1382–1392.                    https://doi.org/10.1039/C8EN01427B                                    ")\], the fact that it takes between 500 and 800 years to degrade, that it releases toxins during this time \[ [73](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR73 "Narancic T, Verstichel S, Reddy Chaganti S, Morales-Gamez L, Kenny ST, De Wilde B, Babu Padamati R, O’Connor KE (2018) Biodegradable plastic blends create new possibilities for end-of-life management of plastics but they are not a panacea for plastic pollution. Environ Sci Technol 52:10441–10452.                    https://doi.org/10.1021/acs.est.8b02963                                    ")\], that its waste is mismanaged and littering occurs \[ [75](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR75 "Wayman C, Niemann H (2021) The fate of plastic in the ocean environment – a minireview. Environ Sci Process Impacts 23:198–212.                    https://doi.org/10.1039/D0EM00446D                                    ")\], coupled with the single-use culture and its release into the environment, has a negative impact \[ [73](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR73 "Narancic T, Verstichel S, Reddy Chaganti S, Morales-Gamez L, Kenny ST, De Wilde B, Babu Padamati R, O’Connor KE (2018) Biodegradable plastic blends create new possibilities for end-of-life management of plastics but they are not a panacea for plastic pollution. Environ Sci Technol 52:10441–10452.                    https://doi.org/10.1021/acs.est.8b02963                                    ")\]. During the manufacturing of plastic, some compounds, that are toxic to the environment, do not react to form plastic. These compounds are discarded as waste material into the environment \[ [76](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR76 "Esterhuizen M, Kim YJ (2022) Effects of polypropylene, polyvinyl chloride, polyethylene terephthalate, polyurethane, high-density polyethylene, and polystyrene microplastic on Nelumbo nucifera (Lotus) in water and sediment. Environ Sci Pollut Res 29:17580–17590.                    https://doi.org/10.1007/s11356-021-17033-0                                    ")\].
  
  Per individual, an average of 12 000 single use sanitary products can be used and disposed of during a woman’s reproductive years, of which each item may take 800 years to degrade \[ [3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR3 "Hand J, Hwang C, Vogel W, Lopez C, Hwang S (2023) An exploration of market organic sanitary products for improving menstrual health and environmental impact. J Water Sanit Hyg Dev 13:63–77.                    https://doi.org/10.2166/washdev.2023.020                                    ")\]. The sanitary pads of ~ 2 million women would fill the Empire State Building. Plastic-based disposable sanitary pads are ~ 90% non-degradable material, and includes the wrapper, the adhesive strip, the plastic back sheet, etc. \[ [3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR3 "Hand J, Hwang C, Vogel W, Lopez C, Hwang S (2023) An exploration of market organic sanitary products for improving menstrual health and environmental impact. J Water Sanit Hyg Dev 13:63–77.                    https://doi.org/10.2166/washdev.2023.020                                    "), [77](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR77 "Hameed MSA, Sreedharan SP, Sivapragasam P, Chakraborty S, Devarajulu C, Sivagami K (2024) Resource recovery from soiled sanitary napkin waste—a state-of-the-art review. Environ Sci Pollut Res 31:30336–30352.                    https://doi.org/10.1007/s11356-024-33218-9                                    ")\]. The product itself is not the only thing that impacts the environment negatively, the manufacturing of the components that make up the sanitary pads also impact the environment negatively with its emissions from material acquisition, manufacturing, processing, packaging, and transportation. The manner of disposal based on culture and socio-economic status can cause other problems such as blocked sewage pipes, rapid filling of landfill sites, exposure of plastic to livestock, release of microplastic into water sources and the soil, the leaching of chemicals into the environment, and growth of pathogens. Therefore, the use of organic based products would resolve this range of issues as biodegradation time of these sanitary products can be reduced from 800 years to just approximately 5 weeks \[ [3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR3 "Hand J, Hwang C, Vogel W, Lopez C, Hwang S (2023) An exploration of market organic sanitary products for improving menstrual health and environmental impact. J Water Sanit Hyg Dev 13:63–77.                    https://doi.org/10.2166/washdev.2023.020                                    ")\].
  
  In 2010, 192 coastal countries generated 4.6–12.6 million tons of plastic that ended up in the ocean \[ [73](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR73 "Narancic T, Verstichel S, Reddy Chaganti S, Morales-Gamez L, Kenny ST, De Wilde B, Babu Padamati R, O’Connor KE (2018) Biodegradable plastic blends create new possibilities for end-of-life management of plastics but they are not a panacea for plastic pollution. Environ Sci Technol 52:10441–10452.                    https://doi.org/10.1021/acs.est.8b02963                                    ")\]. In South Africa, the plastic consumption per capita is 30–50 kg per year which totals to approximately 1.8–3 million tons. Plastic is the waste material with the lowest recycling rate among other recyclables, such as tyres, glass, paper, and metals. This results in the accumulation of plastics on landfills. Furthermore, 19.7 million South African citizens (34.1% of the population) do not have waste collection services, resulting in illegal dumping and burning of their waste \[ [78](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR78 "Mazhandu ZS, Muzenda E, Belaid M, Mamvura TA, Nhubu T (2021) A review of plastic waste management practices what can South Africa learn? Adv Sci Technol Eng Syst J 6:1013–1028.                    https://doi.org/10.25046/aj0602116                                    ")\].
  
  Plastics are susceptible to abiotic and biotic degradation processes. Abiotic degradation processes include hydrolytic, thermal, oxidative, mechanical, and photodegradation. Figure [13](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Fig13) shows the abiotic degradation of polyethylene \[ [79](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR79 "Chamas A, Moon H, Zheng J, Qiu Y, Tabassum T, Jang JH, Abu-Omar M, Scott SL, Suh S (2020) Degradation rates of plastics in the environment. ACS Sustain Chem Eng 8:3494–3511.                    https://doi.org/10.1021/acssuschemeng.9b06635                                    ")\]. Photodegradation, the most efficient degradation process, is when the plastic is exposed to UV and visible light, which it absorbs, promoting the oxidation of the polymers that result in polymer chain scissions. Since abiotic degradation causes loss in structural and mechanical integrity of the plastic, surface irregularities are created, facilitating the colonization of microorganisms, which secrete extracellular enzymes that can alter the physicochemical properties of the surface of the plastic (biotic degradation process) \[ [74](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR74 "González-Pleiter M, Tamayo-Belda M, Pulido-Reyes G, Amariei G, Leganés F, Rosal R, Fernández-Piñas F (2019) Secondary nanoplastics released from a biodegradable microplastic severely impact freshwater environments. Environ Sci Nano 6:1382–1392.                    https://doi.org/10.1039/C8EN01427B                                    ")\]. These processes cause plastics to break into smaller pieces (smaller than 5 mm) called microplastics. Microplastics go on and break into even smaller pieces (smaller than 1000 nm) called nanoplastics \[ [74](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR74 "González-Pleiter M, Tamayo-Belda M, Pulido-Reyes G, Amariei G, Leganés F, Rosal R, Fernández-Piñas F (2019) Secondary nanoplastics released from a biodegradable microplastic severely impact freshwater environments. Environ Sci Nano 6:1382–1392.                    https://doi.org/10.1039/C8EN01427B                                    "), [75](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR75 "Wayman C, Niemann H (2021) The fate of plastic in the ocean environment – a minireview. Environ Sci Process Impacts 23:198–212.                    https://doi.org/10.1039/D0EM00446D                                    ")\]. The negative effects of nanoplastics on aquatic organisms that have been investigated show that it affects the growth, development, behaviour, reproduction, and mortality of filter feeders, fish, and algae \[ [74](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR74 "González-Pleiter M, Tamayo-Belda M, Pulido-Reyes G, Amariei G, Leganés F, Rosal R, Fernández-Piñas F (2019) Secondary nanoplastics released from a biodegradable microplastic severely impact freshwater environments. Environ Sci Nano 6:1382–1392.                    https://doi.org/10.1039/C8EN01427B                                    "), [80](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR80 "Della Torre C, Bergami E, Salvati A, Faleri C, Cirino P, Dawson KA, Corsi I (2014) Accumulation and embryotoxicity of polystyrene nanoparticles at early stage of development of sea urchin embryos Paracentrotus lividus. Environ Sci Technol 48:12302–12311.                    https://doi.org/10.1021/es502569w                                    "), [81](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR81 "Besseling E, Wang B, Lürling M, Koelmans AA (2014) Nanoplastic affects growth of S. obliquus and reproduction of D. magna. Environ Sci Technol 48:12336–12343.                    https://doi.org/10.1021/es503001d                                    ")\]. It can also interfere with hormone signalling, causing oxidative stress and inflammation \[ [75](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR75 "Wayman C, Niemann H (2021) The fate of plastic in the ocean environment – a minireview. Environ Sci Process Impacts 23:198–212.                    https://doi.org/10.1039/D0EM00446D                                    ")\].
  
  **Figure 13**
  
  ![Figure 13](https://media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1007%2Fs10853-025-11151-7/MediaObjects/10853_2025_11151_Fig13_HTML.png)The alternative text for this image may have been generated using AI.
  
  [Full size image](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7/figures/13)
  
  The abiotic degradation products of PE \[ [75](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR75 "Wayman C, Niemann H (2021) The fate of plastic in the ocean environment – a minireview. Environ Sci Process Impacts 23:198–212.                    https://doi.org/10.1039/D0EM00446D                                    ")\].
  
  Since nanoplastics have a large surface area to volume ratio, it can absorb toxic compounds better and have the potential to transfer these toxic compounds to the organism that has ingested it \[ [75](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR75 "Wayman C, Niemann H (2021) The fate of plastic in the ocean environment – a minireview. Environ Sci Process Impacts 23:198–212.                    https://doi.org/10.1039/D0EM00446D                                    ")\]. Nanoplastics can be transferred through the algae-crustacean-fish food chain with negative effects on the organism on each trophic level \[ [82](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR82 "Cedervall T, Hansson L-A, Lard M, Frohm B, Linse S (2012) Food chain transport of nanoparticles affects behaviour and fat metabolism in fish. PLoS ONE 7:e32254.                    https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0032254                                    "), [83](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR83 "Mattsson K, Johnson EV, Malmendal A, Linse S, Hansson L-A, Cedervall T (2017) Brain damage and behavioural disorders in fish induced by plastic nanoparticles delivered through the food chain. Sci Rep 7:11452.                    https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-017-10813-0                                    ")\]. Mattsson _et.al_. revealed that nanoplastics have the potential to cross the blood brain barrier in fish, and that it accumulated in vital organs \[ [83](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR83 "Mattsson K, Johnson EV, Malmendal A, Linse S, Hansson L-A, Cedervall T (2017) Brain damage and behavioural disorders in fish induced by plastic nanoparticles delivered through the food chain. Sci Rep 7:11452.                    https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-017-10813-0                                    ")\]. Furthermore, it has been documented that nanoplastics ingested by humans induced inflammatory responses, caused oxidative stress, and accumulated in organs that may be hazardous to human health \[ [84](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR84 "Lehner R, Weder C, Petri-Fink A, Rothen-Rutishauser B (2019) Emergence of nanoplastic in the environment and possible impact on human health. Environ Sci Technol 53:1748–1765.                    https://doi.org/10.1021/acs.est.8b05512                                    ")\]. Therefore, it is evident that the effects of plastic pollution are more extensive than what we anticipate.
  
  Perez _et. al._ reviewed articles written in the last 20 years to estimate the amount of absorbent hygiene products that have become waste in different countries, and to analyse the management and environmental impact of these products \[ [85](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR85 "Velasco Perez M, Sotelo Navarro PX, Vazquez Morillas A, Espinosa Valdemar RM, Hermoso Lopez Araiza JP (2021) Waste management and environmental impact of absorbent hygiene products: a review. Waste Manag Res: J Sustain Circ Econ 39:767–783.                    https://doi.org/10.1177/0734242X20954271                                    ")\]. It was clear that developed countries in general has a specific waste management system for hygienic absorbent products (baby nappies, adult nappies, and disposable sanitary pads), while in most developing countries, it is handled and disposed of with general household waste and ends up on landfills. Some methods of managing absorbent hygiene product (AHP) waste include valorisation, recycling, biodegradation, thermal processes, and landfilling \[ [85](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR85 "Velasco Perez M, Sotelo Navarro PX, Vazquez Morillas A, Espinosa Valdemar RM, Hermoso Lopez Araiza JP (2021) Waste management and environmental impact of absorbent hygiene products: a review. Waste Manag Res: J Sustain Circ Econ 39:767–783.                    https://doi.org/10.1177/0734242X20954271                                    ")\].
  
  The environmental impact of commercial AHPs was compared to more eco-friendly versions of the commercially available ones \[ [85](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR85 "Velasco Perez M, Sotelo Navarro PX, Vazquez Morillas A, Espinosa Valdemar RM, Hermoso Lopez Araiza JP (2021) Waste management and environmental impact of absorbent hygiene products: a review. Waste Manag Res: J Sustain Circ Econ 39:767–783.                    https://doi.org/10.1177/0734242X20954271                                    ")\]. The factors with the most significant environmental impact of the commercially available AHPs included the manufacturing process, waste management of the SAPs used in the AHPs, and fossil fuel depletion to produce plastics (polyethylene etc.). The factors with the most significant environmental impact of the eco-friendly commercially available AHPs included fertilizers, herbicides, pesticides, and energy used in the cultivation of cotton \[ [85](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR85 "Velasco Perez M, Sotelo Navarro PX, Vazquez Morillas A, Espinosa Valdemar RM, Hermoso Lopez Araiza JP (2021) Waste management and environmental impact of absorbent hygiene products: a review. Waste Manag Res: J Sustain Circ Econ 39:767–783.                    https://doi.org/10.1177/0734242X20954271                                    ")\].
  
  More environmentally friendly alternatives to synthetic disposable sanitary pads are being produced such as reusable cloth pads, menstrual cups, bamboo fibre pads, banana fibre pads, and water hyacinth pads \[ [86](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR86 "Kaur R, Kaur K, Kaur R (2018) Menstrual hygiene, management, and waste disposal: practices and challenges faced by girls/women of developing countries. J Environ Public Health 2018:1–9.                    https://doi.org/10.1155/2018/1730964                                    ")\]. There is a strong drive in ‘going green’ to decrease the environmental impact of technologies and industries. Not only is it for the benefit of the environment, but also for the improvement of the health of women, as synthetic material not only often contain toxic chemicals, but it can also create moist and warm environments for infections to occur (yeast and bacterial infections) \[ [87](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR87 "Shibly MDMH, Hossain MA, Hossain MF, Nur MG, Hossain MB (2021) Development of biopolymer-based menstrual pad and quality analysis against commercial merchandise. Bull Natl Res Cent 45:50.                    https://doi.org/10.1186/s42269-021-00504-2                                    ")\]. Therefore, research has been moving in the direction of producing renewable and biodegradable plastics to substitute current fossil-derived plastics. Alginate \[ [88](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR88 "Paixão LC, Lopes IA, Barros Filho AKD, Santana AA (2019) Alginate biofilms plasticized with hydrophilic and hydrophobic plasticizers for application in food packaging. J Appl Polym Sci 136:48263.                    https://doi.org/10.1002/app.48263                                    ")\], lignan \[ [89](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR89 "Wang H-M, Yuan T-Q, Song G-Y, Sun R-C (2021) Advanced and versatile lignin-derived biodegradable composite film materials toward a sustainable world. Green Chem 23:3790–3817.                    https://doi.org/10.1039/D1GC00790D                                    ")\], starch \[ [90](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR90 "Amin MdR, Chowdhury MA, Kowser MdA (2019) Characterization and performance analysis of composite bioplastics synthesized using titanium dioxide nanoparticles with corn starch. Heliyon 5:e02009.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.heliyon.2019.e02009                                    ")\], cellulose \[ [91](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR91 "Azmin SNHM, Hayat NABM, Nor MSM (2020) Development and characterization of food packaging bioplastic film from cocoa pod husk cellulose incorporated with sugarcane bagasse fibre. J Bioresour Bioprod 5:248–255.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jobab.2020.10.003                                    ")\], chitosan \[ [92](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR92 "Priyadarshi R, Rhim J-W (2020) Chitosan-based biodegradable functional films for food packaging applications. Innov Food Sci Emerg Technol 62:102346.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ifset.2020.102346                                    ")\], polylactic acid (PLA) \[ [93](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR93 "Naser AZ, Deiab I, Darras BM (2021) Poly(lactic acid) (PLA) and polyhydroxyalkanoates (PHAs), green alternatives to petroleum-based plastics: a review. RSC Adv 11:17151–17196.                    https://doi.org/10.1039/D1RA02390J                                    ")\], polycaprolactone (PCL), polybutylene succinate (PBS) \[ [94](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR94 "Rafiqah SA, Khalina A, Harmaen AS, Tawakkal IA, Zaman K, Asim M, Nurrazi MN, Lee CH (2021) A review on properties and application of bio-based poly(butylene succinate). Polymers (Basel) 13:1436.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/polym13091436                                    ")\], and polyhydroxyalkanoates (PHA) \[ [93](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR93 "Naser AZ, Deiab I, Darras BM (2021) Poly(lactic acid) (PLA) and polyhydroxyalkanoates (PHAs), green alternatives to petroleum-based plastics: a review. RSC Adv 11:17151–17196.                    https://doi.org/10.1039/D1RA02390J                                    ")\] are among the biodegradable polymers that have been studied as possible substitutes for plastic films.
  
  Biodegradability is an attractive characteristic for any form of plastic that will most likely end up in the environment one day; however, it can be used to our advantage when it can be degraded in a composting facility \[ [95](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR95 "Lambert S, Wagner M (2017) Environmental performance of bio-based and biodegradable plastics: the road ahead. Chem Soc Rev 46:6855–6871.                    https://doi.org/10.1039/C7CS00149E                                    ")\]. It is also important to consider that some biodegradable material may not always degrade in environmental conditions but can be compostable, such as polylactic acid (PLA). A plastic material’s composting ability depends on its material properties such as polymer type, crystallinity, the additives in the material, and the conditions of the composting environment (moisture, temperature, pH, microbial activity). For instance, soil has more microbial communities than the ocean, therefore; the low temperature and low microbial communities of the ocean will cause biodegradation to be minimal \[ [95](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR95 "Lambert S, Wagner M (2017) Environmental performance of bio-based and biodegradable plastics: the road ahead. Chem Soc Rev 46:6855–6871.                    https://doi.org/10.1039/C7CS00149E                                    ")\].
  
  Currently, the superabsorbent cores of pads consist mostly of sodium polyacrylates integrated with cellulosic fibres \[ [87](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR87 "Shibly MDMH, Hossain MA, Hossain MF, Nur MG, Hossain MB (2021) Development of biopolymer-based menstrual pad and quality analysis against commercial merchandise. Bull Natl Res Cent 45:50.                    https://doi.org/10.1186/s42269-021-00504-2                                    ")\]. The absorbency of this type of core is high; however, sodium polyacrylates do not readily degrade in the environment \[ [87](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR87 "Shibly MDMH, Hossain MA, Hossain MF, Nur MG, Hossain MB (2021) Development of biopolymer-based menstrual pad and quality analysis against commercial merchandise. Bull Natl Res Cent 45:50.                    https://doi.org/10.1186/s42269-021-00504-2                                    ")\]. Several alternatives for sodium polyacrylates have been investigated, such as sodium alginate, carboxy methyl cellulose, cellulose based hydrogels \[ [87](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR87 "Shibly MDMH, Hossain MA, Hossain MF, Nur MG, Hossain MB (2021) Development of biopolymer-based menstrual pad and quality analysis against commercial merchandise. Bull Natl Res Cent 45:50.                    https://doi.org/10.1186/s42269-021-00504-2                                    ")\], cotton, linen fibres, hemp fibres, bamboo fibres \[ [96](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR96 "Foster J, Montgomery P (2021) A study of environmentally friendly menstrual absorbents in the context of social change for adolescent girls in low- and middle-income countries. Int J Environ Res Public Health 18:9766.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/ijerph18189766                                    ")\], and banana fibres \[ [97](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR97 "Sareen S (2021) Sustainable menstrual alternatives: the journey so far. Int J Home Sci 7:216–219.                    https://doi.org/10.13140/RG.2.2.34990.43842                                    "), [98](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR98 "Achuthan K, Muthupalani S, Kolil VK, Bist A, Sreesuthan K, Sreedevi A (2021) A novel banana fiber pad for menstrual hygiene in India: a feasibility and acceptability study. BMC Womens Health 21:129.                    https://doi.org/10.1186/s12905-021-01265-w                                    ")\].
  
  Hand _et. al._ reviewed the impact that plastic based commercial pads have on human health and on environmental health, and alternative plant-based environmentally friendly materials that can be manufactured at low cost. They tabulated some of the commercial disposable sanitary pads that have been produced up to date, with the main absorbent layer being organic (see Table [6](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Tab6)) \[ [3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR3 "Hand J, Hwang C, Vogel W, Lopez C, Hwang S (2023) An exploration of market organic sanitary products for improving menstrual health and environmental impact. J Water Sanit Hyg Dev 13:63–77.                    https://doi.org/10.2166/washdev.2023.020                                    ")\]. Each pad created was developed for different reasons, to address certain social, environmental, and economic issues, with one common goal: affordable, eco-friendly, hygienic pad that is an alternative to expensive plastic-based pads. The materials used to make the sanitary pads are native to the area it is made in, can be locally harvested, and resolve issues of waste management of certain components of plants, such as the banana stems used to make banana fibre, that would otherwise be burnt after banana harvests \[ [3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR3 "Hand J, Hwang C, Vogel W, Lopez C, Hwang S (2023) An exploration of market organic sanitary products for improving menstrual health and environmental impact. J Water Sanit Hyg Dev 13:63–77.                    https://doi.org/10.2166/washdev.2023.020                                    ")\]. The organic biodegradable sanitary pads that are available on the South African market are listed in Table [7](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Tab7).
  
  **Table 6 Commercial organic disposable sanitary pads**
  
  [Full size table](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7/tables/6)
  
  **Table 7 Commercial biodegradable pads available in South Africa**
  
  [Full size table](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7/tables/7)
  
  There are three factors that play a role on the environmental impact of disposable sanitary pads: the regulations created and implementation of the regulations set out by the government in the proper disposing of sanitary pads, the environmental impact of the manufacturing of pads and the availability of pads after manufacturing, and lastly the awareness of menstrual health management (organic vs. inorganic pads, disposal, etc.) \[ [99](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR99 "Choudhary J, Bhattacharjee M (2018) A study on consumption pattern of sanitary napkin and environment degradation. AISECT Univ J 15:906–911")\]. Choudhary _et. al._ conducted research on the sanitary pad market in Delhi, India, and discovered that there is a fourth key factor that plays a role in the environmental impact of disposable sanitary pads; the price of sanitary pads. The reason the environmental impact of disposable sanitary pads is so large in India is not because the Acts set out by the Indian Government with regards to sanitary pad disposal is inadequate, or the awareness of women in India about the environmental impact of disposable sanitary pads is not lacking. It is because synthetic pads (sold under the brand “Whisper”) are the leaders in sanitary pad sales in India, due to its low cost (3.75 rupees per pad). The leading biodegradable pad on the market, Saathi, is only available to the middle- and high-income women of India due to much higher prices (22.25 rupees per pad). Therefore, price is the first barrier of using eco-friendly pads \[ [99](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR99 "Choudhary J, Bhattacharjee M (2018) A study on consumption pattern of sanitary napkin and environment degradation. AISECT Univ J 15:906–911")\].
  
  Choudhary _et. al._ also compared the impact of the manufacturing processes of synthetic and biodegradable pads \[ [99](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR99 "Choudhary J, Bhattacharjee M (2018) A study on consumption pattern of sanitary napkin and environment degradation. AISECT Univ J 15:906–911")\]. They evaluated factors such as nanoplastics creation, by-product waste generation, bleach, greenhouse gas pollution, and the release of dioxins. The raw material of the biodegradable pad, cotton, require, at worst, pesticides and fertilizers where the synthetic pads require chemical processing and generates by-product waste. Therefore, the impact of the manufacturing process of synthetic pads on the environment is much greater \[ [99](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR99 "Choudhary J, Bhattacharjee M (2018) A study on consumption pattern of sanitary napkin and environment degradation. AISECT Univ J 15:906–911")\].
  
  In a study by Gayathri _et. al._, the degradation rate of synthetic disposable sanitary pads was evaluated by bacterial and fungal isolates found in dump yard soil (in vitro test) and by different types of soil (agricultural, garden, and sewage sludge soil) to simulate environmental conditions \[ [100](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR100 "Gayathiri B, Gayathiri V (2018) Formulation of bacterial and fungal isolates for the degradation of sanitary napkins in in vitro conditions. Asian J Microbiol Biotechnol Environ Sci 20:S230–S236")\]. Results showed that a bacterial isolate found in dump site soil, had the highest degradation rate and sewage sludge soil performed best due to the presence of many microorganisms in the soil that collectively degrade the disposable sanitary pads. In vitro degradation was better than the soil degradation test, suggesting that synthetic disposable sanitary pads can be degraded under a controlled bacterial or fungal environment. However, this study did not investigate whether this type of degradation produces micro or nanoplastics and its impact on soil conditions \[ [100](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR100 "Gayathiri B, Gayathiri V (2018) Formulation of bacterial and fungal isolates for the degradation of sanitary napkins in in vitro conditions. Asian J Microbiol Biotechnol Environ Sci 20:S230–S236")\].
  
  Mismisuraya _et. al._ studied the possible degradation of disposable sanitary pads with oyster mushrooms as a way of removing them from the environment \[ [101](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR101 "Mismisuraya MA, Yaacob ND, Zulkifle NAN (2021) Biotic degradation of plastic hygiene products by using Pleurotus ostreatus. IOP Conf Ser Earth Environ Sci 765:012015.                    https://doi.org/10.1088/1755-1315/765/1/012015                                    ")\]. Oyster mushrooms can degrade cellulosic material; thus, the researchers studied what would happen to the plastic attached to the cellulose. They tested the degradability of disposable sanitary pads and diapers by exposing them to oyster mushroom growth and analysed the tensile reduction, chemical, and morphology characteristics. After 90 days of exposure to oyster mushrooms, the absorbent products used showed weight loss, degradation of the surface, and changes in the plastic’s chemical properties. The results suggest that oyster mushrooms have the potential to degrade plastics in absorbent hygiene products \[ [101](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR101 "Mismisuraya MA, Yaacob ND, Zulkifle NAN (2021) Biotic degradation of plastic hygiene products by using Pleurotus ostreatus. IOP Conf Ser Earth Environ Sci 765:012015.                    https://doi.org/10.1088/1755-1315/765/1/012015                                    ")\].
  
  ### Economic impact of absorbent hygiene products
  
  Statistics South Africa reported in 2011 that 45.5% of South Africans are classified as being “poor”, meaning they are unable to purchase necessary food items \[ [102](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR102 "Mills L, Howe C (2019) That time of the month: discrimination against girl children who cannot afford sanitary health care. In: Skivenes M, Sovig KH (eds) Child rights and international discrimination law : implementing article 2 of the United Nations convention on the rights of the child. Routledge, London, p 228")\]. They also reported that 36.4% of South Africans live under the Lower Bound Poverty Line (LBPL), meaning for them to afford non-food items, they would have to sacrifice buying some food items. In 2015, it was reported that 40.0% of South Africans lived below the LBPL, where adult females (41.7%) experienced poverty at higher levels than their male counterparts (38.2%) \[ [103](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR103 "Statistics South Africa (2019) Sustainable Development Goals: Country report 2019. Pretoria")\].
  
  In a developing country like South Africa, young girls entering puberty are not given many choices when it comes to addressing their menses. Only recently, locally produced disposable sanitary pads have entered the market; otherwise, they only have a choice between imported brands which are often too expensive for families to buy. In a country like Uganda, one tenth of a family’s salary is equivalent to the cost of 1 month’s supply of disposable sanitary pads for one woman \[ [104](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR104 "Musaazi MK, Mechtenberg AR, Nakibuule J, Sensenig R, Miyingo E, Makanda JV, Hakimian A, Eckelman MJ (2015) Quantification of social equity in life cycle assessment for increased sustainable production of sanitary products in Uganda. J Clean Prod 96:569–579.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jclepro.2013.10.026                                    ")\]. In South Africa, the poorest 70% of households require an average of US$ 19.12 per woman to meet their sanitary product needs; however, reported expenditure of these households indicate that they only meet 8% of their sanitary needs \[ [105](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR105 "Independent Panel of Experts for the Review of Zero Rating in South Africa (2018) Recommendations on Zero Ratings in the Value-Added Tax System")\]. It is estimated that a third of South African females cannot afford disposable sanitary pads, forcing them to use, often less hygienic, alternatives. Therefore, one-third of school going girls miss school monthly and experience a great deal of anxiety, embarrassment, and discomfort \[ [102](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR102 "Mills L, Howe C (2019) That time of the month: discrimination against girl children who cannot afford sanitary health care. In: Skivenes M, Sovig KH (eds) Child rights and international discrimination law : implementing article 2 of the United Nations convention on the rights of the child. Routledge, London, p 228")\]. This proves that there is inadequate access to commercial disposable sanitary pads in South Africa due to its economic costs.
  
  In Uganda, there was a need for a sanitary pad that is more affordable with the same or more effective absorption and retention abilities of commercial disposable sanitary pads. Therefore, Dr Moses Kizza Musaazi developed MakaPads \[ [104](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR104 "Musaazi MK, Mechtenberg AR, Nakibuule J, Sensenig R, Miyingo E, Makanda JV, Hakimian A, Eckelman MJ (2015) Quantification of social equity in life cycle assessment for increased sustainable production of sanitary products in Uganda. J Clean Prod 96:569–579.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jclepro.2013.10.026                                    ")\]. This locally produced sanitary pad has created job opportunities for 250 Ugandans, most of them women, where imported pads would usually only benefit citizens who transport them and sell them. In addition to job creation, the MakaPad costs approximately half of the price of imported pads in Uganda \[ [104](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR104 "Musaazi MK, Mechtenberg AR, Nakibuule J, Sensenig R, Miyingo E, Makanda JV, Hakimian A, Eckelman MJ (2015) Quantification of social equity in life cycle assessment for increased sustainable production of sanitary products in Uganda. J Clean Prod 96:569–579.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jclepro.2013.10.026                                    ")\]. Thus, it not only benefits the local economy but also provides an affordable solution for menstrual hygiene management \[ [103](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR103 "Statistics South Africa (2019) Sustainable Development Goals: Country report 2019. Pretoria")\].
  
  Therefore, economically, a project like the MakaPads project in Uganda, could benefit other low-income countries in similar ways. A locally produced pad can break the economic inequality of access to sanitary products, it can improve the economic status of not only for the women in low-income countries, but the labour force in general, and have far reaching affects into other areas of inequality and menstrual health.
  
  ### Socio-economic impact of absorbent hygiene products
  
  The lack of access to disposable sanitary pads, whether due to a lack of education or funds, has a tremendous impact on the rest of a women’s life, as it can affect their decision to go to school during the time of menstrual flow \[ [102](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR102 "Mills L, Howe C (2019) That time of the month: discrimination against girl children who cannot afford sanitary health care. In: Skivenes M, Sovig KH (eds) Child rights and international discrimination law : implementing article 2 of the United Nations convention on the rights of the child. Routledge, London, p 228")\]. In developing countries, like South Africa, this decision is further deterred by the fact that some schools do not have proper, private sanitation facilities. Some girls may stay away from school for a total of 3 months per year \[ [102](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR102 "Mills L, Howe C (2019) That time of the month: discrimination against girl children who cannot afford sanitary health care. In: Skivenes M, Sovig KH (eds) Child rights and international discrimination law : implementing article 2 of the United Nations convention on the rights of the child. Routledge, London, p 228")\], and in the rest of Africa, this is the reality for as many as 66% of school girls \[ [96](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR96 "Foster J, Montgomery P (2021) A study of environmentally friendly menstrual absorbents in the context of social change for adolescent girls in low- and middle-income countries. Int J Environ Res Public Health 18:9766.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/ijerph18189766                                    ")\]. Therefore, girls who cannot afford to manage their menses effectively, are not only denied an education but also denied an opportunity to develop their personalities, mental and physical abilities, and talents \[ [102](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR102 "Mills L, Howe C (2019) That time of the month: discrimination against girl children who cannot afford sanitary health care. In: Skivenes M, Sovig KH (eds) Child rights and international discrimination law : implementing article 2 of the United Nations convention on the rights of the child. Routledge, London, p 228")\]. This infringes so many rights of school girls, including the right to social security, non-discrimination, education, dignity, and health. Moreover, not having access to sanitary wear may put girls at risk to be more susceptible to infections. Even if they do have access to sanitary wear, there is always anxiety involved with worrying about leakage, especially if they use poor quality sanitary wear \[ [102](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR102 "Mills L, Howe C (2019) That time of the month: discrimination against girl children who cannot afford sanitary health care. In: Skivenes M, Sovig KH (eds) Child rights and international discrimination law : implementing article 2 of the United Nations convention on the rights of the child. Routledge, London, p 228")\].
  
  A survey conducted in South Africa found that 92% of the participants preferred disposable menstrual products, with 69% preferring disposable sanitary pads \[ [106](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR106 "Nhlapo M, Mashego M, Low M, Ming D, Harding K (2019) Investigating the development of low-cost sanitary pads. Procedia Manuf 35:589–594.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.promfg.2019.05.083                                    ")\]. Of the participants, 10% felt that menstrual products were too expensive, and some commented on the need for environmentally friendly disposable sanitary pads. Nhlapo _et. al._ performed maximum capacity tests, absorption rate tests, and moisture retention tests on their pad prototypes and on Always, Kotex, and Comfitex brands \[ [106](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR106 "Nhlapo M, Mashego M, Low M, Ming D, Harding K (2019) Investigating the development of low-cost sanitary pads. Procedia Manuf 35:589–594.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.promfg.2019.05.083                                    ")\]. The study revealed that the most preferred brand, Always, is the most expensive (R2.50-R2.62 per unit) and performed the best, and that the cheapest brand is Comfitex (R1.70-R2.20 per unit). Even though their prototypes performed well compared to the other brands, the quality of the sanitary pads itself was not good \[ [106](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR106 "Nhlapo M, Mashego M, Low M, Ming D, Harding K (2019) Investigating the development of low-cost sanitary pads. Procedia Manuf 35:589–594.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.promfg.2019.05.083                                    ")\]. A low-cost and environmentally friendly form of sanitary napkins should be produced to fit the socio-economic structure of the end-user. Cultural acceptability and the end-user's socioeconomic status will determine the sanitary pad they choose. However, there are other factors, such as absorbency and comfort of the materials that also play a role \[ [107](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR107 "Mburu AW, Kinyanjui J (2013) Development of a highly absorbent and antibacterial biodegradable sanitary pad from bamboo. In: National Council For Science And Technology 2nd National Science, Technology And Innovation Week. Nairobi")\].
  
  Women resort to using improvised, and often unhygienic menstrual products when they cannot afford to buy disposable sanitary pads \[ [8](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR8 "Kambala C, Chinangwa A, Chipeta E, Torondel B, Morse T (2020) Acceptability of menstrual products interventions for menstrual hygiene management among women and girls in Malawi. Reprod Health 17:185.                    https://doi.org/10.1186/s12978-020-01045-z                                    ")\]. These improvised products often leak, they cause foul smells, and when not cleaned properly, lead to infections causing not only physical and financial stress, but psychological stress as well. These factors eventually cause women and girls to stay at home and manage their menses at home, to avoid embarrassment, anxiety, and shame \[ [8](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR8 "Kambala C, Chinangwa A, Chipeta E, Torondel B, Morse T (2020) Acceptability of menstrual products interventions for menstrual hygiene management among women and girls in Malawi. Reprod Health 17:185.                    https://doi.org/10.1186/s12978-020-01045-z                                    ")\]. These feelings are experienced globally, because even though societies and cultures have different ideas about menstruation, a study revealed that over 5000 terms exist to refer to menstruation, many of which are profane and disparaging \[ [108](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR108 "Dave U, Palaniappan A, Lewis E, Gosine B (2022) An overview of period poverty and the public health benefit impact of providing free feminine hygiene products. Int J Health Serv Res Policy 7:221–226.                    https://doi.org/10.33457/ijhsrp.971839                                    ")\]. This puts strain on the education of scholars and students, on women’s social and family life, and work \[ [8](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR8 "Kambala C, Chinangwa A, Chipeta E, Torondel B, Morse T (2020) Acceptability of menstrual products interventions for menstrual hygiene management among women and girls in Malawi. Reprod Health 17:185.                    https://doi.org/10.1186/s12978-020-01045-z                                    ")\]. To compound these stresses and anxiety young girls experience, some cultures have traditions and perception that adds to their stress. For example, in the northern region of Malawi, girls would not only miss school, but they would be forced by the family to stay indoors, take baths by a stream, not in the house, and to use a different bathroom than the rest of the family, when they experience their first period. Community members would notice when women are menstruating when they are using cloths as the thick rags used would be noticeable under one’s clothes and fouls smells would follow them. During menstruation, girls are not allowed to speak to males (men or boys) in their community, they are not allowed to cook, and not allowed to pass some areas, such as gardens, as menstruation is believed to have the power to curse plants \[ [8](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR8 "Kambala C, Chinangwa A, Chipeta E, Torondel B, Morse T (2020) Acceptability of menstrual products interventions for menstrual hygiene management among women and girls in Malawi. Reprod Health 17:185.                    https://doi.org/10.1186/s12978-020-01045-z                                    ")\]. Another issue is that girls are ashamed to hang their menstrual cloths out in the sun, which is required as the sunlight has antimicrobial action _;_ therefore, they rather put it in cupboards or keep them inside, which poses many health problems \[ [109](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR109 "Pagore P, Chaudhari S (2021) Menstrual hygiene management: a literature review. J Pharm Res Int.                    https://doi.org/10.9734/jpri/2021/v33i60B34881                                    ")\].
  
  Menstruation is an important phase in a women’s life, as it actually symbolizes good health and her ability to give birth \[ [110](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR110 "Chaturvedi B, Goswami S, Pal N, Singh R, Yadav T, Gangwar S, Mishra K, Kumudhavalli M (2021) A brief note on menstrual stigma: social assumptions and responsibilities. Int J Curr Res Rev 13:60–63.                    https://doi.org/10.31782/IJCRR.2021.13604                                    ")\]. However, many cultures believe it symbolises that the woman is impure, sinful, and that whatever she touches will spoil. They are not allowed to enter religious spaces. It is shameful to discuss this phenomenon with males, even if they suspect serious problems or issues \[ [110](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR110 "Chaturvedi B, Goswami S, Pal N, Singh R, Yadav T, Gangwar S, Mishra K, Kumudhavalli M (2021) A brief note on menstrual stigma: social assumptions and responsibilities. Int J Curr Res Rev 13:60–63.                    https://doi.org/10.31782/IJCRR.2021.13604                                    ")\]. In the Malawi study, they found that the reason girls and women avoided using disposable sanitary pads is that they were not sure how to use it, but once they were shown how to use it, they expressed their preference in using it above traditional reusable cloths \[ [8](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR8 "Kambala C, Chinangwa A, Chipeta E, Torondel B, Morse T (2020) Acceptability of menstrual products interventions for menstrual hygiene management among women and girls in Malawi. Reprod Health 17:185.                    https://doi.org/10.1186/s12978-020-01045-z                                    ")\]. Traditional cloths require time, a lot of water and soap to clean, and they are deemed “old fashioned” However, some elderly women expressed their concern about disposable sanitary pads, as they felt that the chemicals used in making them would not be good for a woman’s body. Another challenge was availability, as pads were reportedly not available at all shops, and when available, expensive. Because of this, many girls would combine the use of disposable and reusable pads. Both type of users experienced problems in accessing resources to manage their menses. Long distances are to be travelled to the nearest cities to access material to make reusable pads from, or to buy disposable sanitary pads \[ [8](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR8 "Kambala C, Chinangwa A, Chipeta E, Torondel B, Morse T (2020) Acceptability of menstrual products interventions for menstrual hygiene management among women and girls in Malawi. Reprod Health 17:185.                    https://doi.org/10.1186/s12978-020-01045-z                                    ")\].
  
  In India, women are the pillars of society, as they are the providers of care, hygiene, nutrition, and primary healthcare _;_ therefore, health and nutrition depend on these factors, especially on the caregiver at home \[ [111](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR111 "Kumari V, Harvinder M (2020) Socio-economic factors affecting women health: a review. Indian J Health Well Being 11:147–150")\]. Their ability to make decisions in terms of nutrition, cooking, cleaning, and taking care of the children, all depend on the status of the woman. In the rural areas, women are also part of the workforce, bringing home an income. Therefore, the quality of life of women determines how well all these factors perform, if their physical and mental health is not good, their family and society would not perform as it should. Their social–cultural position also plays a role in their acceptance of seeking healthcare. A healthy mind lives in a healthy body. Many of the health problems women in India face is because males and male children are regarded as having more value. Mental health plagues them, especially during pregnancy, and their hygiene practices, more so in rural areas than the urban areas. During menstruation, a study revealed that 54.8% of the participants use homemade sanitary products, which were pieces of cloth. Hygiene practices were only satisfactory for 43.4% of the participants evaluated, and lack of awareness, knowledge, and for some, ignorance, were the main reasons the women were not using disposable sanitary pads. They found that education is linked to mental health and physical health as it results in increased awareness of signs of disease and availability of health services. As well as gender inequality, as healthcare, drugs and medical devices are considered too expensive or not necessary for them and only 49.17% of women had the right to take decisions regarding their healthcare. The relationship the women have with their family also influences their mental and physical health, as the women in a joint family provide support and share knowledge about issues, such as menstrual health \[ [111](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR111 "Kumari V, Harvinder M (2020) Socio-economic factors affecting women health: a review. Indian J Health Well Being 11:147–150")\].
  
  Even in a first world country like America, studies have shown that 21% of women from the low-income sector have trouble in affording feminine hygiene products, and 50% of low-income women must choose between purchasing hygiene products and food \[ [108](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR108 "Dave U, Palaniappan A, Lewis E, Gosine B (2022) An overview of period poverty and the public health benefit impact of providing free feminine hygiene products. Int J Health Serv Res Policy 7:221–226.                    https://doi.org/10.33457/ijhsrp.971839                                    ")\].
  
  ## The current trend in sanitary pad research
  
  Despite the health and environmental implications of disposable sanitary pads, it is still the preferred sanitary hygiene product among menstruaters \[ [45](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR45 "Peberdy E, Jones A, Green D (2019) A study into public awareness of the environmental impact of menstrual products and product choice. Sustainability 11:473.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/su11020473                                    ")\]. In response, research has been focusing on creating AHPs that have natural origin, that are eco-friendly, biodegradable, and biocompatible with the body. The following discussion will highlight some of the promising innovations to the key components of these products: the top sheet, absorbent core, and the back sheet, focusing on sustainable materials and performance of the final product.
  
  ### Current work on the top sheet of sanitary pads
  
  Traditionally, nonwoven polyethylene and polypropylene materials are used as the top sheet of sanitary pads \[ [112](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR112 "Dinu L-D, Iordache O, Vamanu E (2022) Scanning electron microscopy study on the biodeterioration of natural fiber materials compared to disposable hygiene and sanitary products. Fermentation 8:287.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/fermentation8060287                                    ")\]. To replace this non-biodegradable top sheet, many researchers have designed and developed pads where the top sheet have been replaced with nonwoven bamboo \[ [113](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR113 "Mounika K, Ilango KB, Baranitharan AK, Dhivyatharshini S, Gowtham K, Swetha V, Vijaykumar S (2023) Biodegradable sanitary napkins-an innovative approach towards menstrual hygiene using bamboo fiber and lemon grass. Int J Pharm Sci 1:135–139.                    https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.10078084                                    "), [114](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR114 "Jeyakanthan DM, Schuster J, Shaik YP (2023) Development of ecological absorbent core sanitary pads in combination of kenaf and chitosan fibers. Mater Sci Appl 14:363–381.                    https://doi.org/10.4236/msa.2023.147023                                    "), [115](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR115 "Niveda R, Ramakrishnan G (2024) Development of cost-effective, eco-friendly sanitary pads for better health and sanitation of rural women. Funct Text Cloth 2023:289–294")\], nonwoven cotton \[ [116](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR116 "Mukhila R, Amutha K (2023) Eco friendly disposable sanitary napkin development. Int J Res Publ Rev 4:2687–2693"), [117](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR117 "Pasha C, Aravind S, Begum A, Pasha SM (2023) Antimicrobial and biodegradable sanitary pads with nanomaterials fused polymers. Asian J Biol Sci 16:218–228.                    https://doi.org/10.3923/ajbs.2023.218.228                                    ")\], and even with nonwoven cattail fibres \[ [118](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR118 "Dewar-Norosky M, Wyllie-Runner J, Duguay N, Pries M, McRae C (2023) Design of a compostable menstrual pad: fluid acquisition layer and absorbent core. University of Manitoba")\]. The first line of defence against infections and skin irritation in disposable sanitary pads is the type of material used that touches the skin \[ [119](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR119 "Dias YJ, Robles JR, Sinha-Ray S, Abiade J, Pourdeyhimi B, Niemczyk-Soczynska B, Kolbuk D, Sajkiewicz P, Yarin AL (2021) Solution-blown poly(hydroxybutyrate) and ε-poly- <scp>l</scp> -lysine submicro- and microfiber-based sustainable nonwovens with antimicrobial activity for single-use applications. ACS Biomater Sci Eng 7:3980–3992.                    https://doi.org/10.1021/acsbiomaterials.1c00594                                    ")\]. Since plant fibres are non-irritants it is considered a good alternative \[ [114](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR114 "Jeyakanthan DM, Schuster J, Shaik YP (2023) Development of ecological absorbent core sanitary pads in combination of kenaf and chitosan fibers. Mater Sci Appl 14:363–381.                    https://doi.org/10.4236/msa.2023.147023                                    ")\].
  
  However, synthetic fibres with similar properties can also be designed. Dias _et. al._ addressed the problem by creating poly(hydroxybutyrate) (PHB) and ɛ-poly-ʟ-lysine (ɛ-PLL) sub microfiber antimicrobial nonwovens by solution blowing (see Fig. [14](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#Fig14)). Testing showed these nonwovens have good antibacterial properties, biocompatibility, with a soft touch, and sufficient strength (TS: 7–11 MPa, EB: 23–16%) for end-user applications \[ [119](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR119 "Dias YJ, Robles JR, Sinha-Ray S, Abiade J, Pourdeyhimi B, Niemczyk-Soczynska B, Kolbuk D, Sajkiewicz P, Yarin AL (2021) Solution-blown poly(hydroxybutyrate) and ε-poly- <scp>l</scp> -lysine submicro- and microfiber-based sustainable nonwovens with antimicrobial activity for single-use applications. ACS Biomater Sci Eng 7:3980–3992.                    https://doi.org/10.1021/acsbiomaterials.1c00594                                    ")\]. Poly(lactic acid) polymers have also been studied as a biodegradable alternative for nonwoven materials \[ [120](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR120 "Zhu F, Su J, Wang M, Hussain M, Yu B, Han J (2020) Study on dual-monomer melt-grafted poly(lactic acid) compatibilized poly(lactic acid)/polyamide 11 blends and toughened melt-blown nonwovens. J Ind Text 49:748–772.                    https://doi.org/10.1177/1528083718795913                                    ")\].
  
  **Figure 14**
  
  ![Figure 14](https://media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1007%2Fs10853-025-11151-7/MediaObjects/10853_2025_11151_Fig14_HTML.jpg)The alternative text for this image may have been generated using AI.
  
  [Full size image](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7/figures/14)
  
  **Left**: The scanning electron micrograph of solution blown fibres by Dias et. al.\[ [119](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR119 "Dias YJ, Robles JR, Sinha-Ray S, Abiade J, Pourdeyhimi B, Niemczyk-Soczynska B, Kolbuk D, Sajkiewicz P, Yarin AL (2021) Solution-blown poly(hydroxybutyrate) and ε-poly- <scp>l</scp> -lysine submicro- and microfiber-based sustainable nonwovens with antimicrobial activity for single-use applications. ACS Biomater Sci Eng 7:3980–3992.                    https://doi.org/10.1021/acsbiomaterials.1c00594                                    ")\]. Reprinted from the ACS Biomaterials Science and Engineering, 7, Dias YJ, Robles JR, Sinha-Ray S, Abiade J, Pourdeyhimi B, Niemczyk-Soczynska B, Kolbuk D, Sajkiewicz P, Yarin AL, Solution-blown poly(hydroxybutyrate) and ϵ-poly- l -lysine submicro- And microfiber-based sustainable nonwovens with antimicrobial activity for single-use applications, Copyright (2021), with permission from American Chemical Society. **Right**: Polylactic acid fibres used in non-woven material by Zhu et. al \[ [120](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR120 "Zhu F, Su J, Wang M, Hussain M, Yu B, Han J (2020) Study on dual-monomer melt-grafted poly(lactic acid) compatibilized poly(lactic acid)/polyamide 11 blends and toughened melt-blown nonwovens. J Ind Text 49:748–772.                    https://doi.org/10.1177/1528083718795913                                    ")\]. Reprinted from the Journal of Industrial Textiles, 49, Zhu F, Su J, Wang M, Hussain M, Yu B, Han J, Study on dual-monomer melt-grafted poly(lactic acid) compatibilized poly(lactic acid)/polyamide 11 blends and toughened melt-blown nonwovens, Copyright (2020), with permission from Sage Publications.
  
  The use of new technologies such as 3D printing and nanotechnology is exciting and innovative approaches gaining interest in the application of AHPs. Three-dimensional printing is a process of creating three-dimensional objects from a digital file. It involves laying down successive layers of material until the object is formed. This method was used by Zidaric _et. al._ to create a top sheet for baby diapers with polysaccharide polymers (alginate, carboxymethyl cellulose, nanofibrillated cellulose etc.), enhancing the probiotic activity of _Staphylococcus epidermidis_, a beneficial skin bacterium, by promoting the production of short chain fatty acids (SCFA), to help treat diaper rash \[ [121](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR121 "Zidarič T, Gradišnik L, Frangež T, Šoštarič M, Korunič E, Maver T, Maver U (2024) Novel 3D printed polysaccharide-based materials with prebiotic activity for potential treatment of diaper rash. Int J Biol Macromol 269:131958.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ijbiomac.2024.131958                                    ")\]. The three dimensional printing technique allowed them to have precise control of the material’s structure and properties. They created a top sheet according to the method described in previous work \[ [122](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR122 "Milojević M, Harih G, Vihar B, Vajda J, Gradišnik L, Zidarič T, Stana Kleinschek K, Maver U, Maver T (2021) Hybrid 3D printing of advanced hydrogel-based wound dressings with tailorable properties. Pharmaceutics 13:564.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/pharmaceutics13040564                                    "), [123](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR123 "Zidarič T, Milojević M, Gradišnik L, Stana Kleinschek K, Maver U, Maver T (2020) Polysaccharide-based bioink formulation for 3D bioprinting of an in vitro model of the human dermis. Nanomaterials 10:733.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/nano10040733                                    "), [124](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR124 "Maver T, Mastnak T, Mihelič M, Maver U, Finšgar M (2021) Clindamycin-based 3D-printed and electrospun coatings for treatment of implant-related infections. Materials 14:1464.                    https://doi.org/10.3390/ma14061464                                    "), [125](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10853-025-11151-7#ref-CR125 "Milojević M, Gradišnik L, Stergar J, Skelin Klemen M, Stožer A, Vesenjak M, Dobnik Dubrovski P, Maver T, Mohan T, Stana Kleinschek K, Maver U (2019) Development of multifunctional 3D printed bioscaffolds from polysaccharides and NiCu nanoparticles and their application. Appl Surf Sci 488:836–852.                    https://doi.org/10.1016/j.apsusc.2019.05.283                                    ")\]. It was designed to draw moisture away from the skin. Testing showed that this novel material promotes the growth of SCFA and is safe for use on baby skin.
